The **canonical** durable record of **known gaps between what MAANTA claims and
what is true**, with open/closed state per row. Created 2026-07-30 to close FU-1
of `docs/skills/truth-audit-2026-07-30.md`.

A **dated, ranked view of the rows waiting on a ruling** — with each question,
its evidence and its options — is `docs/maanta-decision-queue-2026-08-19.md`.
That file carries no state and closes nothing: it is re-derived, never edited,
and every closure still happens here by ID with a guard named.

"Canonical", not "only": `docs/skills/parity-drift-register-2026-07-30.md` also
exists and is a dated narrative companion, not a state tracker. That split is
itself tracked as **D27** and is not the intended end state — items there should
land here as rows and close by D-number. This paragraph exists because the header
previously said "single durable record" while D27 recorded a second register four
screens below it, which is precisely the claim-vs-reality gap this file is for.

## Why this file exists

Before it, drift was recorded in dated audit documents (`docs/skills/*-audit-*.md`).
Those are good narratives and bad trackers: nothing carries state forward, so a
finding resolved in one audit can be re-raised or forgotten in the next. That is
not hypothetical — it is what happened:

- The Elite-trial "14 vs 30 days" conflict was resolved on 2026-07-29, and a code
  comment still called it open on 2026-07-30 (**D5**).
- The launch offer was frozen in Notion and had never reached the repo mirror at
  all, despite being referenced in passing by another decisions-log entry (**D6**).
- Two invariants this repo described as enforced were not (**D3**, **D9**).

So the register's job is not to be tidy. It is to make *"we already fixed that"*
a checkable claim.

It started earning that before it was even merged. The first draft carried a row
saying `MAANTA_DEMO_MODE` was unverified; while the register sat on a branch, that
verification landed on `main` and turned up three further gaps in the process
(**D17**–**D20**). Rebasing forced the row to be re-checked against reality rather
than shipped as written — which is the entire mechanism, applied to itself.

## The rules

1. **Rows are append-only.** Never delete or renumber a row — close it. The
   history of what was once wrong is the point.
2. **A row may only be `closed` when its evidence names a guard** — a test, a
   migration, or a decisions-log entry: something that would fail or be
   contradicted if the drift came back. `drift-register.test.ts` fails if the
   cited path does not exist, and fails if the only citation is a document the
   row merely relates to.

   Some drift cannot be guarded — a misleading comment, or behaviour that needs
   test infrastructure this repo lacks. Those may still close, but the evidence
   must say **`no guard: <reason>`** so it is a visible choice. The first draft of
   this register did not have that rule, and an open row flipped to `closed`
   passed the suite because it happened to cite a background doc. That hole was
   found by trying it.
3. **`pending-deploy` is not `closed`.** A fix merged to `main` but not yet live
   (an unapplied migration, an unset env var) stays open in substance. This
   distinction exists because "merged" is the exact point at which people stop
   tracking things.
4. **`deferred` needs an owner and a next step**, not just a shrug.
5. Every audit that finds drift adds rows here **before** writing its narrative,
   and closes prior rows by ID rather than re-describing them.

### Status values

| Status | Meaning |
|---|---|
| `open` | The gap exists right now. |
| `pending-deploy` | Fix is merged but not yet live where it counts. Still a gap. |
| `closed` | Fixed, live, and evidenced. |
| `deferred` | Accepted knowingly, with an owner and a trigger for revisiting. |

### Category values

`code-outlier` · `mirror-stale` · `doc-stale` · `db-metadata` · `product-decision` · `prototype-only` · `process`

| D43 | closed | process | 2026-08-01 | Guard integrity | **D38's fix left a narrower version of D38's bug in place.** That row consolidated three private comment-strippers into `maanta-app/src/lib/__tests__/helpers/comment-stripping.ts`, whose `lineCommentAt()` skips a `//` **preceded by a colon** — which repairs `https://` and nothing else. Its docblock stated the limit out loud and argued it was safe, on the grounds that "a false *strip* can only ever make a guard miss something, which the mutation tests in each consumer are there to catch". A guard that misses is not a mitigated failure mode — it is D36 exactly: green while the file contains the string it forbids. And the colon rule still truncates any other literal carrying a double slash: a protocol-relative "//cdn.example.com", a doubled path segment, a regex source held in a string. Each silently deletes the rest of the line before the scan reads it, in a helper that four suites now depend on. The mutation tests cited as the backstop test the *consumers* against planted URLs, so they confirm the colon case and cannot see the others. Found in review of PR #153, independently of D38 | **Closed by making the lexer string-literal aware.** `stripCommentLines()` now tracks single-quote, double-quote and backtick literals with escape handling, so a double slash inside any literal survives regardless of what precedes it, and `lineCommentAt()` is gone as subsumed. Export names and the file path are unchanged, so D38's consumers and citations still resolve. Exercised directly by `maanta-app/src/lib/__tests__/comment-stripping-lexer.test.ts` — first assertion is the wa.me line that started this, then a double slash inside all three quote styles, escaped quotes, a template literal spanning lines, and an unterminated apostrophe that must not swallow the file. Both remaining limits are now stated as limits rather than as safety: regex literals are untracked, and template interpolations count as string interior — both erring toward scanning too much. **Demonstrated rather than argued:** a protocol-relative `href="//wa.me/254700000000"` planted under `src/components/marketing/` now turns the D36 guard red with file and line; run through the colon rule the same line strips to `return <a href="` and the guard regex returns false, so it would have passed. Negative control still green: the same URL inside a genuine `//` comment is ignored. Directory walking moved to `maanta-app/src/lib/__tests__/helpers/source-files.ts` so each helper has one job | eng |

The component's own docblock asserted the opposite, reasoning that production is safe because the fallback returns first — true, and irrelevant to the preview case it overlooked. Guarded by `maanta-app/src/lib/__tests__/scenario-stat.test.ts`, which asserts the render matrix behaviourally and asserts that the guard reads no `NODE_ENV` at all. That second assertion exists because `NODE_ENV` cannot be varied within one vitest process — React binds `react/jsx-dev-runtime` at module load — so the property is checked at its source rather than simulated | eng |

| D45 | closed | process | 2026-08-01 | Guard integrity | `scripts/check-tokens.mjs` was described — in `CLAUDE.md`, in `docs/ops/website-handoff.md` §8 and in the implementation report — as failing the production build if any `{{TOKEN}}` reaches rendered output. Most routes in this app build as `ƒ` (dynamic) and emit **no HTML at build time**, and a token inside a client component ships in a JS chunk rather than in any HTML at all, so two whole classes of token could reach a visitor while the gate printed "clean". Same family as D38 and D43: a check whose reported success did not mean what its callers believed. Served documents keep the original any-token pattern. Proven on both missed classes: a token planted in a client component fails on `.next/static/chunks/app/(marketing)/waitlist/page-*.js`, and one planted in a **server** component on the same dynamic route — which reaches no chunk directory and no prerendered HTML — fails on `.next/server/app/(marketing)/waitlist/page.js`. Coverage went from 47 files to 47 rendered files plus 392 compiled chunks. Source maps are excluded: they retain comments, and `facts.ts` discusses token names in prose. The scan scope itself is guarded by `maanta-app/src/lib/__tests__/check-tokens-scope.test.ts` — the script fails CI on any token in scope, but nothing else would notice the scope being narrowed back | eng |

Both now hold. Production is `dpl_4Zq5F3UK4QgpmeThCQzbgLqCrfqz`, `state: READY`, `target: production`, `githubCommitRef: main`, `githubCommitSha: b6f19716`, holding `www.maanta.app` and `maanta.app`; and `git merge-base --is-ancestor b6f19716 origin/main` passes, trivially, because it is `main`'s tip. The guard vacuity that made this row urgent was separately fixed and is covered by D38, whose remedy — one shared comment lexer at `maanta-app/src/lib/__tests__/helpers/comment-stripping.ts` imported by every copy guard — is what stops the underlying defect returning. | D38 | closed | code-outlier | 2026-08-01 | Testing | `maanta-app/src/lib/__tests__/held-claims.test.ts` is cited across this repo as the guard that keeps §9 held claims out of shippable content, but its `codeOnly()` helper strips line comments with `.replace(/\/\/.*$/gm, "")` — which truncates every line from the first `//`, including the `//` inside any `https://` URL. Any held claim sharing a line with a URL is therefore invisible to the scanner. This is the identical defect that `038e3bc0` fixed in `maanta-app/src/lib/__tests__/marketing-shell.test.ts` and `maanta-app/src/lib/__tests__/pricing-copy.test.ts`; that commit patched two of the three files carrying it and left this one, on both branches. Neither the gap audit nor the finish plan records it | **Closed 2026-08-01.** `lineCommentAt()` ported into `maanta-app/src/lib/__tests__/held-claims.test.ts`, and `codeOnly()` rewritten to strip line comments line-by-line through it instead of by blanket regex — so the fix covers all three assertions the helper feeds, not just the `HELD`-pattern scan. **Verified by mutation in both directions, against both trees.** Positive: a held claim (`anything left in your balance stays yours`) planted on the *same line as an `https://` link* — the exact shape the old stripper deleted before scanning — turns the guard red; **the identical mutation passes 5/5 against `origin/main`'s copy of the file**, which is the blindness demonstrated rather than argued. Negative controls, both still green so the fix does not over-correct: the same claim inside a `{/* … */}` JSX comment, and after a genuine `//` line comment, are each still correctly ignored. De-vacuuming it revealed **no** hidden violation — the guard was blind but the content behind it was clean, the same outcome as the two files `038e3bc0` fixed. Suite 481/481 after the change. **The triplication is also gone**, in the follow-up change: the one implementation now lives in `maanta-app/src/lib/__tests__/helpers/comment-stripping.ts` and all three guards import it, removing 131 lines of duplicated lexer for 19. A fourth private copy is how this defect recurs, and it was found twice — once by `038e3bc0`, once as this row — so the duplication is the root cause rather than a tidiness point. The helper is deliberately not named `*.test.ts`, because the suite glob in `maanta-app/vitest.config.ts` matches only that suffix and would otherwise collect the helper and run it as an empty suite; the count stays 61 files / 481 tests. Its docblock records the trap and states the scope limit out loud (a small lexer, not literal-aware — a `//` inside a quoted string is still treated as a comment start, which can only ever make a guard miss, never false-positive). **Re-verified after the extraction, since it refactors already-verified guards:** four positive mutations all red (hardcoded `wa.me`; `KES 40 success fee`; a held claim on a line with a link; `Boost any deal` in JSX) and four negative controls all green (each of those planted inside a `{/* */}` or `//` comment, including a `wa.me` URL *inside* a comment — which is the both-directions case, since the stripper must ignore `//` in `https://` while still honouring a genuine `//` comment that contains a URL) | eng |

| D39 | open | doc-stale | 2026-08-01 | Marketing site | `docs/ops/marketing-site-gap-audit.md` GAP-03 records `/how-it-works` as a live **rewrite** serving `/shoppers` at a second URL, on the evidence of an HTTP 200 with `x-matched-path: /shoppers` and a byte-identical body. The repo says otherwise: `maanta-app/next.config.mjs` declares `{ source: "/how-it-works", destination: "/shoppers", permanent: true }` inside `redirects()`, which is a 308, and `rewrites()` contains only the three PostHog `/ingest/*` proxy rules. The audit's observations are also exactly what a **followed** 308 looks like to a redirect-following HTTP client, and the PostHog pageviews it cites are explained by `/how-it-works` having been a real page until the marketing rebuild (`docs/skills/e2e-surface-matrix-2026-07-30.md`). So either the finding is a measurement artifact or something outside the repo is intercepting the path |  Resolve with `curl -sI` and redirect-following **off**: a 308 closes this row and closes finish-plan Step 3 with no code change; a genuine 200 means the cause is outside `maanta-app/next.config.mjs` and must be found in the Vercel project configuration before anything is edited. **Do not edit the redirect on the strength of the audit alone.** Reasoning in `docs/ops/marketing-site-repo-map.md` §4. **Partial progress 2026-08-02, and a correction to an earlier attempt at closing this row.** This row was briefly marked `closed` on the reasoning below and is **reverted to `open`**, because that reasoning contained an error caught in review on #169. **What is now established, and is not in doubt:** the repository's compiled behaviour is a 308. The generated `routes-manifest.json` at `.next` lists `/how-it-works` → `/shoppers` with `statusCode: 308`, `how-it-works` appears in the `rewrites` array zero times, there is no `vercel.json` in the tree, and `maanta-app/src/middleware.ts` does not mention the path. That is guarded going forward by `maanta-app/src/lib/__tests__/marketing-redirects.test.ts` — see **D57**, which stands on its own and is closed. **What is also established:** the audit's *evidence* for GAP-03 is uninformative. Fetching production reproduced the audit's observation for `/how-it-works` (HTTP 200, no `location`, `x-matched-path: /shoppers`) and reproduced it identically for `/for-shoppers` and `/for-merchants`, which are undisputed 308 redirects, with `x-matched-path` tracking each one's own destination. A followed 308 and a rewrite are indistinguishable under this measurement, so the audit's 200 supports neither conclusion. **The error, stated plainly because it was asserted with confidence:** the first closure argued that the rewrite hypothesis "would additionally require `/for-shoppers` and `/for-merchants` to be rewrites too". That does not follow. A Vercel-side rewrite on `/how-it-works` **alone** is entirely consistent with the other two being ordinary redirects; the control shows the measurement cannot distinguish, not that the hypothesis is excluded. Sufficiency of the redirect explanation is not exclusion of the rewrite one. **What remains open, and why this row does:** nothing available to a Claude session can rule out routing configured outside the repository. The network policy refuses the CONNECT tunnel to `www.maanta.app` with the proxy's own 403 — the D42 constraint — the Vercel fetch tool that can reach the host follows redirects, and `get_project` does not expose project-level routing. **Next step, unchanged from the original remedy:** one unfollowed `curl -sI https://www.maanta.app/how-it-works` from an ordinary terminal. A 308 with `location: /shoppers` closes this row; a genuine 200 means the cause is outside `maanta-app/next.config.mjs` and must be found in the Vercel project configuration. Recorded rather than resolved silently, per the register's own rule about conflicts between documentation and production evidence. → **T4 sweep 2026-08-19:** **repo side unchanged and still settled; the measurement is still owed.** `maanta-app/next.config.mjs` declares `{ source: "/how-it-works", destination: "/shoppers", permanent: true }` inside `redirects()`, and `rewrites()` still holds only the PostHog proxies. The `curl -sI` with redirect-following off was **not** performed this session: this environment's egress proxy refuses `maanta.app` directly (HTTP 403), and the Vercel fetch that works instead follows redirects, so it cannot answer the question this row asks | eng |

| D40 | closed | code-outlier | 2026-08-01 | Marketing site | Not one page on the marketing site emits `<link rel="canonical">` — there is no `alternates` key anywhere under `maanta-app/src/app/(marketing)/`. Separately, `maanta-app/src/app/layout.tsx` sets `openGraph.url` to the bare site origin rather than each page's own URL, so every share of a sub-page canonicalises its unfurl to the homepage; and because a page-level `openGraph` object **replaces** the parent's wholesale in the App Router rather than merging field-by-field, the five pages that declare their own lose `og:url`, `og:site_name`, `og:locale` and `og:type` entirely. The site therefore claims per-page social identity it does not have. `metadataBase` is already correctly set, so this is narrower than it first appears | Add a shared metadata helper setting `alternates.canonical` and spreading the shared `openGraph` base before per-page overrides, and route all 15 marketing pages through it. The helper must read the same `NEXT_PUBLIC_APP_URL` fallback constant as `maanta-app/src/app/layout.tsx`, or preview deployments will emit production canonicals. **Closed 2026-08-01 (finish-plan Step 4).** `maanta-app/src/lib/marketing/page-metadata.ts` builds every marketing page's `Metadata`: `alternates.canonical` plus a shared `openGraph` base (`type`, `siteName`, `locale`) spread in **before** the page's overrides, so a page can restyle its social title without silently dropping the other four fields. **All seventeen** marketing `page.tsx` files now go through it — enumerated, not sampled: `find src/app/(marketing) -name page.tsx` returns seventeen, and all seventeen call `pageMetadata`. Two of them reached this row by a different path than the one originally written here. `/pricing` had no metadata at all when this fix was authored and `/merchants/join` was a `"use client"` module that could not export any; both were fixed independently in the intervening work (**D52**, and the `/pricing` card rebuild), so this pass wraps metadata they already had rather than creating it. The `/merchants/join` server/client split therefore predates this change — `maanta-app/src/app/(marketing)/merchants/join/join-form.tsx` already existed, mirroring the `waitlist` shape, and is unchanged here. `path` is passed **relative** and resolved against the root layout's `metadataBase`, so this module holds no second copy of the origin and a preview deployment cannot emit production canonicals. GAP-05 fixed in the same pass: `/mall-operators` and `/about` no longer split one sentence across `og:title` and `og:description`, both halves recombined from each page's own approved copy. **Verified in built HTML, not source** — 16 prerendered routes each carry a canonical and `og:url` equal to their own URL, with all three OG base fields; `/waitlist` is dynamically rendered so it has no build artefact to read and is reported by name rather than skipped. Guarded by `maanta-app/scripts/check-canonicals.mjs`, chained into `npm run build` beside the token gate — **not** a vitest suite, because CI runs `test` before `build` and a test reading `.next/` would fail there. It derives its expectations from the generated sitemap, so "sitemap URL and page canonical agree" is enforced rather than coincidental, and it refuses to report success when it cannot do its job: missing build dir, truncated sitemap, or fewer than 14 routes inspected all exit 1, and it asserts in the inverse direction that the 404 has **no** canonical. Mutation-tested four ways — canonical removed, `og:url` reverted to the bare origin, `og:site_name` dropped, canonical wrongly added to the 404 — each turns it red; and two vacuity controls (absent build dir, sitemap with zero pages) make it refuse rather than pass, which is the specific failure D38 and the three strippers before it were made of. **The wiring is guarded too**, by `maanta-app/src/lib/__tests__/build-gates.test.ts`: a post-build gate is only as durable as the `build` script that invokes it, and deleting the `&& npm run check:canonicals` would have disabled it silently with a green suite and a green build. That test asserts both output gates exist, are named in `build`, run **after** `next build`, and are joined with `&&` rather than `;` so a failure actually fails the build — mutation-tested by unchaining one and by re-joining with `;`. It also closes the same hole for `check-tokens.mjs`, which had shipped unguarded in exactly that way since it was written | eng |

Verified in a browser on the marketing routes, the only ones reachable without auth: no icon renders dimensionless anywhere (`noSize: 0`), and the header logomark now renders at its requested 28px instead of 20px, which is a visible improvement against the wordmark. **What keeps this row open:** the merchant, shopper and ops surfaces cannot be rendered from a Claude session because they sit behind auth, and they include the counter-side money screens — `redeem-keypad`, `topup-flow`, `redemption-result`, `tickets/[id]` — where icons grow 20px to 32px. Close by citing a human confirmation that those were viewed. no guard: no test can assert a rendered pixel size on an authenticated route; CI runs no browser for unit tests, and the e2e golden path does not cover icon geometry. → **T4 sweep 2026-08-19:** **still accurate.** `maanta-app/src/components/ui/icons.tsx` carries the per-axis predicate (`!setsDimension(className, "h") && "h-5"`), so the code fix is in place exactly as the row describes, and the row is open for the stated non-code reason: the merchant, shopper and ops surfaces cannot be rendered from a Claude session | eng |

| D55 | closed | doc-stale | 2026-08-01 | Marketing site | The two documents that exist to correct the finish plan both misdescribe `/waitlist`, and they misdescribe it in the direction that sets a trap. `docs/ops/marketing-site-repo-map.md` §6 says `waitlist-form.tsx` "prerenders because it is a client component that **does not** call `useSearchParams()`", and **D41**'s own remedy column repeats it as the in-repo precedent to follow. `/waitlist` does not prerender. `maanta-app/src/app/(marketing)/waitlist/page.tsx` accepts a `searchParams` prop to preset the segment, and accepting `searchParams` opts a route into dynamic rendering — it is `ƒ /waitlist` in every build, which is exactly why `scripts/check-canonicals.mjs` has to report it as having no build artefact to inspect. The generalised lesson those documents draw is right and is what Step 5 applied; the sentence supporting it is wrong about the shipped route. The trap is specific: a reader told "copy `/waitlist`, it prerenders" would copy the `searchParams` prop, get a form in the HTML, see the defect fixed, and not notice they had turned `/contact` dynamic and dropped it out of the canonical guard's coverage — trading a visible defect for an invisible one | **Closed 2026-08-01, in the same pass that closed D41 and by not falling into it.** Step 5 took the alternative the repo map names one paragraph later — read the parameter in an effect — precisely because that keeps the prerender, and `/contact` is still `○` in the build output. Both statements corrected in place: `marketing-site-repo-map.md` §6 now separates the component (which would prerender on its own) from the route (which does not, because of the page around it), and D41's remedy text is left as written with the closure text naming the correction, per the append-only rule. `maanta-app/scripts/check-server-forms.mjs` states the fact in its own docblock and lists `/waitlist` under `DYNAMIC_WITH_FORMS`, printing it on every build, so the next reader meets it as build output rather than as prose. no guard: the drift is a sentence in a correction document, and no test can assert that prose describes a route accurately. The nearest machine-checkable proxy already exists in both build gates — each names `/waitlist` as having no artefact on every run — and would start disagreeing loudly with the corrected prose only if `/waitlist` were made static, which is a change worth noticing anyway | eng |

| D56 | open | process | 2026-08-01 | Release | **Second manual promote of an open PR branch to production in one day, and the control named in D53 is what failed.** Deployment `dpl_ALPrV9RBGrbncco8YE2bzpsF7jMd` was created with `action: promote`, `target: production`, `source: redeploy`, from `githubCommitRef: claude/install-superpowers-plugin-na574p` at `githubCommitSha: 602323e` — an **open** PR (#166) — and took the aliases `www.maanta.app` and `maanta.app`. D53 recorded the first occurrence and closed it `no guard`, reasoning that "production deploys are driven by `main` pushes by default, so this only recurs when a human promotes a branch by hand". That reasoning was correct and the control was still insufficient: it recurred within roughly four hours. **This occurrence was materially worse than D53's.** There the promoted tree and `main`'s tree were the identical object, so the site served correct code throughout and only provenance was wrong. Here the trees genuinely differed — deployed `a959d669`, `main` `ed3b3d4d`, two commits apart — so `main` did not contain the `/contact` fix that production was serving. Any push to `main`, or any deploy triggered from it, would have silently reverted `www.maanta.app` to the state D41 describes: a contact page shipping zero `<form>` elements beneath its own promise of a 1-business-day reply. The exposure window was **18:31:47Z to 18:38:47Z** — the promote's `createdAt` to the replacement deployment reaching `READY` and taking the production aliases. Both endpoints are recorded read-backs. An earlier draft of this row ended the window "at the merge", which the evidence does not support: no merge timestamp was ever captured, and the merge is in any case the wrong boundary — exposure ends when production stops serving the promoted deployment, not when the button is pressed. Note also that the divergence was **not** detectable from the PR: GitHub showed a normal preview deployment, and only the Vercel comment's encoded `previewUrl` — `www.maanta.app` rather than the branch alias — gave it away | **Incident resolved 2026-08-01, but this row stays open because what is unresolved is the control, not the incident.** Resolved the same way D53 was, on the author's instruction: merged #166 rather than rolling back, since rolling back would have reverted the `/contact` fix. The merge pushed `225db23` to `main` and auto-triggered `dpl_HiNU4vwbuhTJfEwoS5uUfPmLs3Xk` with `githubCommitRef: main`, `target: production` — verified by reading the deployment, not inferred from the merge. Tree equality confirmed rather than assumed: `main` and the promoted deployment both resolve to `a959d669`. **Why this is not closed:** D53 closed with a stated control, reality tested that control the same day, and it did not hold. Closing this row on the same reasoning would assert the recurrence risk is handled when nothing has changed to handle it, which is precisely the "we already fixed that" failure this register exists to prevent. What would actually close it is a configuration decision, not a code change and not a document: either disabling manual production promotes for this project, or restricting promote to deployments built from `main`. That is a founder call about a Vercel project setting, so no session can take it. **Until then the operational rule is:** if a branch must go live before merge, merge it — a promote from an open branch is a production state no test in this repo can see, and the only reason all three occurrences were caught is that a session happened to decode a Vercel bot comment. Also worth recording for whoever audits this: compare **trees, not commit SHAs**, since a squash merge mints a new SHA and an ancestry check against a promoted branch commit fails forever even when the content is identical (the D53 note), and conversely identical SHAs are not what proves agreement — tree equality is. **Evidence artifact: `docs/ops/production-branch-promote-2026-08-01.md`**, which holds the full Vercel read-backs for all three occurrences, the tree comparison with its commands, the timeline, and a copy-paste procedure for checking what production is actually serving. It exists because the evidence for this row lives outside the repository — a Vercel deployment record ages out and a session transcript is not a durable record — so without it a future audit of D53 or D56 has nothing to check. **Third occurrence 2026-08-02 07:36Z, recorded in §8 of that artifact: `dpl_6zKSsfbFeVWtrjpvbfKuSvSuEaUz`, promoted from `205a7ed` on PR #167 — the pull request whose only content was the documentation of this problem.** Harmless in itself: the trees differed but the whole diff was two documentation files, nothing under `maanta-app/`, so the rendered site was byte-identical to `main` — established with `git diff --name-only origin/main <sha> -- maanta-app/`, which is the check that separates "trees differ" from "the site is wrong" and should be the first one run next time. Resolved by merging #167. **Three occurrences in under 24 hours, one of which put a shipped fix at risk, and writing the incident down did not change the behaviour** — which is the strongest available evidence that what is missing is the Vercel setting named above and not more prose. Two of the three were harmless, and that is the trap rather than the reassurance: a failure mode that is usually harmless is one people learn to tolerate, and the harmful one was the middle of the three. If promoting the working branch is the intended workflow rather than an accident, this row should be **rewritten as a deliberate practice with its risks named** rather than left as an incident awaiting a control that will never be applied; what is not sustainable is a documented incident class with no control and a recurrence rate of once every eight hours. → **T4 sweep 2026-08-19:** **no recurrence in the current window, and nothing prevents one.** The three most recent `target: production` deployments on `maanta-nuia`, read from Vercel 2026-08-19, are all from `githubCommitRef: main` — `5040acda`, `a6bc30cd`, `95374d6b` — with no branch promote among them. That is evidence the pattern has not recurred lately, not evidence it cannot: the control this row and **D71** both name as absent is still absent | founder |

| D57 | closed | doc-stale | 2026-08-02 | Marketing site | Two findings from closing **D39**, both about the three legacy marketing redirects — `/for-shoppers`, `/for-merchants` and `/how-it-works`. **First, the code comment stated the wrong status code.** The docblock above `redirects()` in `maanta-app/next.config.mjs` opened "These are 301s because the old paths are printed on flyers", and had said so since the redirects were written. `permanent: true` in Next.js emits **308**, not 301 (`permanent: false` emits 307), and the generated `routes-manifest.json` — at the root of `.next`, not under `.next/server` — confirms `statusCode: 308` on all three. The distinction is not pedantry — a 301 permits a client to rewrite POST to GET when following, a 308 requires the method and body to be preserved — and the comment is load-bearing precisely because it explains a decision the next reader is meant to respect rather than revisit. **Second, and larger: nothing asserted the redirects existed at all.** The comment itself names the reason they matter — these paths are on flyers, in WhatsApp groups and on in-mall signage (risk R6), inbound links nobody can edit after the fact — yet deleting a row from `redirects()` would have broken every printed link with a green suite and a green build. The one guard the marketing site had for this was prose in a document that was itself wrong | **Both closed 2026-08-02.** The comment is corrected in place and now states 308, why it is not 301, and that the claim was verified against the build manifest rather than inferred from the config. Guarded by `maanta-app/src/lib/__tests__/marketing-redirects.test.ts`, which imports `next.config.mjs` and calls `redirects()` — testing the values Next will compile rather than source text matched with a regex, which is possible here because the config imports cleanly through the Sentry wrapper. It asserts each source's destination and `permanent: true`, and — in the inverse direction — that none of the three appears in `rewrites()`, which is the assertion that would have settled GAP-03 in a second rather than a day. It also pins `rewrites()` to the PostHog `/ingest/*` proxy only, since a rewrite serves foreign content at our own paths and every addition should be deliberate. **Mutation-tested four ways:** deleting the `/how-it-works` redirect fails it, flipping `permanent` to `false` fails it, changing the destination fails it, and converting the redirect into a rewrite — the exact GAP-03 hypothesis — fails three assertions including the inverse-direction one. A first assertion checks the config actually loaded, so the rest cannot pass against an empty array. Deliberately **not** reading the generated `routes-manifest.json`, where the literal `statusCode: 308` lives: CI runs `test` before `build`, so a test reading `.next/` would fail there and one that skipped on a missing directory would pass vacuously every run — the manifest was read by hand when D39 closed, and `permanent: true` → 308 is Next's documented mapping. The manifest is referred to by name rather than cited as a repo path on purpose: it is build output, not committed, so it is not evidence a reader of this repo can open — the same standard D56 was held to | eng |

| D58 | closed | doc-stale | 2026-08-02 | Money path | `docs/maanta-project-overview.md` §Technical state asserted "**Money movements** all flow through the `record_merchant_ledger_entry` RPC — atomic, idempotent by provider reference." At least four functions INSERT into `merchant_transactions` without going near that RPC: `purchase_boost` (`maanta-app/supabase/migrations/20260715194145_boost_elite_only_gate.sql:22`), `activate_merchant` writing the Node 0 opening credit (`20260716084804_node0_opening_credit_on_activation.sql:50`), `reverse_success_fee` (`20260722120000_admin_fee_reversal_wallet_credit.sql:114`) and `deduct_success_fee_or_record_arrears` (`20260630232014_arrears_support.sql:27`), which is what `verify_redemption` calls to move the KES 30 fee. The decisions log says so in as many words — the 2026-07-16 entry states the opening credit is "**Not** routed via `record_merchant_ledger_entry` (service_role-only), mirroring `purchase_boost`" — so the overview contradicts the authority it mirrors. The exposure is an engineer trusting the overview and concluding one RPC is the only place fee, boost and promo money is written | **Closed 2026-08-02** by restating the invariant the code actually holds: money is written only by sanctioned SECURITY DEFINER RPCs, of which `record_merchant_ledger_entry` is the app-layer one, and the four in-database writers are named. `docs/skills/payments-rails.md` §"The one rule" already scoped its version of this claim to `recordMerchantTransaction`, the TypeScript helper, and is the fuller statement — the overview now defers to it. no guard: no test can assert that a prose summary of the money path is complete; the behavioural guard is that every one of those four writers is covered by a SQL suite in `maanta-app/supabase/tests/` (`fee_reversal_test.sql`, `node0_opening_credit_test.sql`, `verify_redemption_money_path_test.sql`, `topup_settles_arrears_test.sql`) | eng |

The middleware does no such thing unconditionally — `maanta-app/src/middleware.ts:14-18` calls `authStrategy()` and returns `updateSession(request)` (Supabase session refresh) for the `supabase` and `authjs` strategies, reaching `clerkHandler` only otherwise. This is D48's shape one layer down: D48 added Clerk to `CLAUDE.md`, and the sentence it added overstated it | **Doc half fixed 2026-08-02**: both files now state the toggle and name the default, pointing at `docs/ops/auth-strategies.md`. **Row stays open on the half that is not a docs question.** `docs/maanta-decisions-log.md` (2026-07-28) — the authority for intent — reads "`clerk` (default, launch ...) vs `supabase` (dev/staging rehearsal ...)", and the code makes `supabase` the default. Either the log means "default for production", in which case the log should say so, or the intended fallback is Clerk and `DEFAULT_AUTH_STRATEGY` is the outlier — a fail-safe question, since the two answers differ in what a misconfigured deployment serves. Founder or eng to rule; the docs-only audit that opened this row had no authority to change either the code or a frozen log entry. no guard yet: the guard is whichever value is ruled correct being asserted in `maanta-app/src/lib/__tests__/` against `DEFAULT_AUTH_STRATEGY`. → **New evidence 2026-08-19, row stays open — the founder question is unchanged.** Production measured at 19:37 UTC via the Vercel fetch (plain `curl` to `www.maanta.app` is refused by this environment's egress proxy, the method note D99 left): `https://www.maanta.app/login` returns HTTP 200 with `x-matched-path: /login/[[...sign-in]]`, embeds `pk_live_Y2xlcmsubWFhbnRhLmFwcCQ` (payload `clerk.maanta.app$`), loads clerk-js from `https://clerk.maanta.app`, renders `ClerkAuthShell` in the RSC payload, and returns `x-clerk-auth-status: signed-out`. Server-side Clerk middleware and the client shell both engaging means **both** `MAANTA_AUTH_STRATEGY` and `NEXT_PUBLIC_MAANTA_AUTH_STRATEGY` are explicitly `clerk` in Vercel Production, since `isClerkAuth()` requires both. Two corrections to how this evidence has been framed elsewhere: it is **not new** — D99 closed on 2026-08-16 on the same production-instance reading, so "D99's development-instance reading no longer reproduces" restates D99's own closure rather than adding to it; and `CLAUDE.md` already states the position correctly, so the repo is not uniformly wrong. The wording that is wrong is `docs/maanta-decisions-log.md`'s 2026-07-28 entry, and a frozen log entry is not a session's to edit. **Consequence recorded, not acted on:** **D82** (shopper profile phone/email editing) was deferred behind this row, so if D59 resolves to "production is Clerk, explicitly configured, and that is intended", D82 becomes actionable. | D83 | open | code-outlier | 2026-08-10 | Money / payments | **The IntaSend top-up webhook is authenticated by a static shared secret echoed in the request body, not by a signature over the payload — and the idempotency guarantee its own ledger comment states does not hold on the one path that can pass a null reference.** `maanta-app/src/lib/intasend.ts` implements `verifyWebhookChallenge` as a plain equality test against `INTASEND_WEBHOOK_SECRET`, so there is no HMAC over `value` / `api_ref` / `invoice_id`, no timestamp, and no nonce: anyone holding that one static token can mint a fully valid credit for any merchant up to `MAX_TOPUP_AMOUNT` (KES 1,000,000), and the webhook amount is never reconciled against the amount the STK push actually initiated. Separately, `maanta-app/src/app/api/webhooks/intasend/route.ts` sets `providerReference` to null when the payload carries neither `invoice_id` nor `id`, while `maanta-app/supabase/migrations/20260709000151_merchant_transactions_provider_reference_unique.sql` says in its own comment that NULLs stay allowed and do not collide — so `record_merchant_ledger_entry` raises no `unique_violation` and credits the wallet again on every delivery. The header comment in `maanta-app/src/lib/merchant-ledger.ts` states flatly that a duplicate delivery rolls back cleanly on the unique constraint, which is true for Stripe (always a non-null reference) and untrue for this caller. Contrast `maanta-app/src/app/api/webhooks/stripe/route.ts`, which verifies a real HMAC over the raw body read via `request.text()` before parsing. Not reachable anonymously — it needs the secret — and IntaSend is a prepared, not-assumed rail per `docs/skills/payments-rails.md`, which is why this is graded High rather than Critical | Two fixes, both small and independent of the provider question. (1) Refuse to credit without a non-null provider reference: derive it from the `api_ref` the app itself minted at initiation (`maanta-app/src/app/api/topup/route.ts` already puts a `randomUUID()` there), so idempotency is guaranteed per initiation rather than per provider goodwill, and log a webhook failure instead of crediting when it is missing. (2) Confirm with IntaSend whether a payload HMAC is offered and verify the raw body against it, mirroring the Stripe handler. If the provider genuinely offers only the static challenge, treat it as authentication-only and reconcile the credited amount against the initiated top-up before applying it. Founder decision needed on (2) only — whether to gate the rail on a real signature before M-Pesa goes live at the pilot. Deploy-state question this repo cannot answer: whether `INTASEND_WEBHOOK_SECRET` is set in production today. It fails closed when unset, so an unset secret means no exploit and also no working M-Pesa credit. **2026-08-10 — half landed, row stays open.** Fix (1) shipped: `maanta-app/src/app/api/webhooks/intasend/route.ts` now keys the ledger on the app-minted `api_ref` and never on the provider's invoice id, so the reference is always non-null and identical across redeliveries; the mixed `invoice_id ?? api_ref` form was rejected as worse than either alone, since a first delivery carrying an invoice id and a retry without one would key differently and double-credit. Guard: `maanta-app/src/app/api/webhooks/intasend/__tests__/route.test.ts`, which asserts the reference is never null, is stable across redeliveries, and is unchanged by the presence of an invoice id — the three assertions fail against the previous code. Replay and accidental double-credit are closed. Fix (2) is **not** done and is what keeps this row open: `verifyWebhookChallenge` is still a static shared secret echoed in the body with no signature over the payload, so a caller holding that secret can still mint fresh `api_ref` values and credit arbitrary amounts. **2026-08-10 (second update) — the reconciliation half is written but NOT YET LIVE.** `maanta-app/supabase/migrations/20260810120000_pending_topups.sql` adds a `pending_topups` table keyed by the app-minted `api_ref`, `maanta-app/src/app/api/topup/route.ts` records what the merchant asked for before the STK push, and `maanta-app/src/app/api/webhooks/intasend/route.ts` now refuses to credit when there is no pending row, when the merchant id disagrees, or when the amount does not match — each refusal logged to `payment_webhook_failures` rather than dropped. Guards: `maanta-app/src/app/api/webhooks/intasend/__tests__/route.test.ts` (a payload naming KES 1,000,000 against an initiated 500 credits nothing) and `maanta-app/supabase/tests/pending_topups_test.sql` (grants, RLS, constraints, cascade). **Deploy state: APPLIED to production 2026-08-10 15:45 UTC** (founder-authorized, via the Supabase MCP — the D25 recorded-exception route; read-back and smoke-test evidence in `docs/security/security-audit-2026-08-10.md` under Production deployment). Ledger now 88 rows with `20260810120000`/`pending_topups` present, RLS on, both SELECT policies present, `authenticated` INSERT and UPDATE both false, and a self-cleaning smoke test against a demo merchant left zero rows and an unchanged balance. The reconciliation half of this row is therefore closed and live; the row stays open on the signature half alone. Superseded note follows, kept for the record: **the migration was unapplied.** Claude does not apply migrations. `make db-verify` also cannot run here, and the reason is an egress-policy denial rather than a missing tool: a Docker daemon started fine and the Supabase CLI was installed at CI's pinned 2.109.1, but the proxy returns 403 for all three registry CDNs (Docker Hub, GHCR, ECR Public), so `supabase start` cannot pull its images. The SQL was therefore verified in isolation instead: a throwaway local PostgreSQL 16.13 cluster with a minimal scaffold of the objects this file references applied `maanta-app/supabase/migrations/20260810120000_pending_topups.sql` cleanly and passed all four scenarios in `maanta-app/supabase/tests/pending_topups_test.sql`, and the test was confirmed non-vacuous by weakening a grant and watching Scenario A fail. The full chain is verified too: the CI `db-tests` job passed on the first run of PR #187 (job 93487120035), with `maanta-app/supabase/tests/pending_topups_test.sql` named in the log and all four scenarios passing against the real Supabase stack — so ordering against the other 87 migrations and RLS behavior under real JWT claims are both confirmed, which the isolated run could not cover. A human must then apply the migration; until then the webhook still credits unreconciled amounts in production. Rollout note: a push initiated before the migration lands has no pending row, so its callback is refused and logged for manual settlement — the intended trade, chosen over leaving the hole open for anyone who can forge a reference. The signature half remains untouched and is what keeps this row open. → **T4 sweep 2026-08-19:** **partly fixed — two of the three items in the remedy are implemented, and the row reads worse than the code.** Read at HEAD, `maanta-app/src/app/api/webhooks/intasend/route.ts` now (1) keys idempotency on the app-minted `api_ref` rather than the provider's optional `invoice_id`, so the null-reference double-credit this row describes cannot occur — `providerReference: apiRef`, with the reasoning in the file's own comment — and (2) reconciles the webhook amount against the initiated top-up, refusing to credit on a mismatch (`Amount mismatch on ${apiRef}: … Refusing to credit.`) and refusing entirely when no pending top-up matches the `api_ref`. **What remains is exactly the founder question the row already isolates**: authentication is still a static shared secret echoed in the body, with no HMAC over the payload, and anyone holding that token can still mint fresh `api_ref`s — the route says so in a comment. Grade the residual accordingly: it is an authentication question on a prepared-not-assumed rail, not a live double-credit | eng |

| D86 | open | process | 2026-08-10 | Ops / migrations | **The Supabase MCP's `apply_migration` mints its own ledger version, so an MCP apply always diverges from the committed filename — and the repo had no procedure requiring anyone to notice.** The tool takes `name` and `query` only; there is no version parameter, so the version comes from the wall clock of the apply. D24 framed this as a one-off to reconcile. It is not: **every MCP apply so far has needed the same repair** — `20260730180000` and `20260730190000` (2026-08-04, repaired 08-05), `20260807160000` and `20260807161000` (2026-08-08), and `20260810120000` (2026-08-10). Five for five. `docs/ops/supabase-migrations.md` recorded each repair as history but carried no instruction to perform one, so the control was five separate acts of someone happening to check. The stakes are not uniform: the earlier migrations were idempotent (`CREATE OR REPLACE` / `DROP`+`CREATE`) so a replay under a mismatched version was harmless, whereas `20260810120000_pending_topups.sql` is bare `CREATE TABLE` / `CREATE POLICY` and an unrepaired ledger would have made the next `supabase db push` re-run it and **error** | **The 2026-08-10 instance is repaired** — ledger reads `20260810120000`/`pending_topups`, total 88, matching `maanta-app/supabase/migrations/`, read back and recorded in `docs/security/security-audit-2026-08-10.md`. **The row stays open because the instance is not the drift.** What was missing was a standing procedure, now written as §7 of `docs/ops/supabase-migrations.md`: note the filename version before, read back the minted version after, repair before any further apply or push, check idempotency to know how urgent the repair is, and record both versions with the operator and timestamp. Close this row when the next MCP apply has gone through §7 and been recorded — the procedure is new and unexercised, and a runbook step nobody has followed yet is a claim, not a control. The better fix, and the standing preference §7 now states, is to avoid the channel: `supabase db push` keys on the filename and never mints a version. no guard: the divergence happens inside a hosted tool, so nothing in this repo can assert on it; §7 is a procedural control and is deliberately recorded as one. → **T4 sweep 2026-08-19:** **the missing half is now present.** The row stayed open because "the instance is not the drift" — what was missing was a standing procedure, not another repair. `docs/ops/supabase-migrations.md` now carries one: a numbered post-apply sequence (read back the ledger, `update supabase_migrations.schema_migrations` to the repo filename, re-read, check the total against a count of the files in `supabase/migrations/`), an explicit note that a non-idempotent migration's repair is urgent rather than tidy-up, a record-both-versions step, and a stated standing preference for `supabase db push` over the MCP. Whether that is enough to close is a judgement about a process control, which a sweep does not get to make — but the row's factual claim that no instruction existed is no longer true | eng |

The enforcement point the comment now describes — the app-layer 403 `phone_required` before the RPC — is behaviourally guarded by that test (phone-less → 403 and `claim_deal` not called; verified-phone → through). Nothing is saved — the service worker has no cache and no `fetch` handler.** `OfflineBanner` in `maanta-app/src/components/ui/states.tsx` renders the black strip "You're offline — showing saved deals" whenever `navigator.onLine` is false, and it is mounted on both authenticated shells: `maanta-app/src/app/(shopper)/layout.tsx` and `maanta-app/src/app/merchant/(app)/layout.tsx`. `maanta-app/public/sw.js` is 38 lines and registers exactly two listeners, `push` and `notificationclick`. It has no `fetch` handler, opens no Cache Storage, and precaches nothing; no other caching layer exists in the app. So when the strip appears, the page behind it can fetch nothing and any client navigation fails. This is a money-surface honesty failure, not a cosmetic one: the merchant shell carries the same banner, and CLAUDE.md's UI bar requires that a blocked state "say so plainly with a next step" rather than be dressed up. It is also not a new discovery — `docs/skills/frozen-ui-overall-handoff.md` lists it under PWA install quality — but it was never registered, which is how it survived the July UI polish pass and the 2026-08-06 sweep untouched. `docs/ops/tech-stack-deep-dive-2026-07.md` states the true position plainly ("Service worker is **not** an offline/cache PWA"), so the repo already disagrees with itself in prose as well as in code | **Open.** Two fixes of very different size, and they are a founder decision, not an engineering preference. **(a) Copy** — change the strip to what is true ("You're offline — reconnect to load deals"), a one-line diff that makes the state honest and can guard as a string assertion in `maanta-app/src/lib/__tests__/frozen-ui-rules.test.ts`. **(b) Behavior** — implement a caching service worker so the claim becomes true, which is real product work and, for a claimed ticket at a counter, touches the redemption path. Note (b) does not make the current copy correct even after shipping, because a cached ticket still cannot be verified offline: `verify_redemption` is an RPC. Recommended: do (a) now, and treat (b) as scoped product work with its own decision. Next step: founder ruling on which. Status: `docs/ops/pwa-status-2026-08-12.md` **CLOSED 2026-08-12 — fix (a) shipped and guarded; fix (b) is deferred as D95, not done.** Founder instruction 2026-08-12 was explicit: correct the wording, build no offline feature. So the false claim is gone and the capability is unchanged — MAANTA remains an installable online-first shell. `maanta-app/src/components/ui/states.tsx` no longer hardcodes a line. `OFFLINE_MESSAGE` maps a new `OfflineContext` prop to three approved strings — shopper "You're offline. Reconnect to load live deals.", merchant "You're offline. Reconnect before verifying a redemption.", generic "You're offline. Reconnect to continue." — and `maanta-app/src/app/(shopper)/layout.tsx` and `maanta-app/src/app/merchant/(app)/layout.tsx` each pass their own. Context is a prop rather than a route sniff so the component never guesses which shell it is in, and the default is the generic line, which is safe anywhere. **Accessibility improved in the same diff, because the old form was silent.** The banner used to `return null` when online, so the live region and its text were inserted in the same tick — which assistive tech announces unreliably. The region is now mounted always and only its text changes, `aria-live="polite"` so a connectivity flap queues rather than interrupting a merchant mid-verification. Online it renders an unclassed empty wrapper, so neither shell's flex column changes height and no styling, layout or routing moved. **Guard: `maanta-app/src/components/ui/__tests__/offline-banner.test.ts`.** Three ratchets, because the copy alone is escapable: the three strings asserted verbatim; each string tested against nine offline-claim patterns; and every `.tsx` under `src/` plus every `.md` under `src/content/` scanned for those patterns, so the promise cannot reappear on an empty state, a toast or a legal page while this component stays clean. It matches claim phrases, not the word "offline", which is legitimate throughout (the DOM event, `navigator.onLine`, the component's own state). Comments are stripped first via the shared D38 lexer, so the docblock may keep quoting the old copy to explain why it is gone. **Proven to fail before being trusted:** reinstating "You're offline — showing saved deals" as the shopper string failed all three ratchets — the verbatim assertion, the `/saved deals/i` pattern, and the file scan reporting `states.tsx:109`. Restored, then re-run green. Ran on the final tree: `npm run lint` clean, `npm run typecheck` clean, `npm test` 85 files / 657 tests passing, `npm run build` passing with all three post-build gates (`check:tokens` 47 rendered files + 401 chunks, `check:canonicals` 16 routes, `check:forms` 2 routes). | D97 | closed | code-outlier | 2026-08-14 | Core loop / Shopper UX | **A shopper whose claim had committed could be told the network failed, in wording that invited a retry.** Two composing defects, found while investigating the incident that became D96: `maanta-app/src/app/(shopper)/deals/[id]/claim-flow.tsx` parsed the response body inside the fetch `try` and before checking `res.ok`, with a `catch` that never inspected what it caught — so every non-JSON response (platform 500, function timeout, empty body) rendered as "Network error — please try again."; and the claim route ran an unbounded what3words call plus three further round trips between the committed `claim_deal` insert and the HTTP response, so a slow provider could convert a committed claim into a user-visible failure. Check My Deals before trying again."; post-claim enrichment runs under a hard 1200ms deadline with every failure swallowed, and the what3words call is bounded (1.5s claim path / 5s default) with an `AbortController` fallback for unpinned runtimes; `deal_not_active` → 410 and session failures → typed 401 routing to login. | D105 | closed | product-decision | 2026-08-15 | Money / merchant wallet | **Design Brief v1.4 in Notion specifies a new-merchant wallet state for the Node 0 opening credit, with exact copy; the wallet renders no such state, and the D104 fix did not add one.** The brief's §9 wallet state table carries the row "New merchant — opening credit / Balance shown, the ledger's first row is the credit, never call it a fee waiver" with exact copy "KES 300 starting credit — your first 10 verified redemptions covered; thereafter a transparent KES 30 success fee." Reality at `d08e94c`: `maanta-app/src/app/merchant/(app)/wallet/page.tsx` renders exactly three `InlineAlert` states — arrears, empty, low balance — and no string matching "starting credit" or "verified redemptions covered" exists anywhere in `maanta-app/src`. So the programme's D-a instinct was pointing at something real, just not where it said: the ledger row exists and is now labelled (D104, closed), while the *state* the brief specifies is genuinely absent. Note the brief's own open-questions section asks for exactly this — item 10 requests ledger row types for the boost, the Elite subscription and the opening credit, of which D104 settled one | Needs a founder ruling before implementation, on two points, which is why this is a row and not a diff. First, the specified copy asserts "your first 10 verified redemptions covered" — that is KES 300 divided by the KES 30 success fee, a derived claim, and hardcoding the 10 would violate the never-hardcode-a-fee rule while the credit and fee both live in `app_config`. Second, the trigger is unspecified: whether the state shows while the credit is unspent, until the first redemption, or only when the credit row is the ledger's sole entry. Notion wins for product copy per the source-of-truth order, so the wording is the brief's to give, not this repo's to invent. → **Closed 2026-08-15 — founder ruled the count derived, and the state shipped the same day.** The brief's sentence is kept clause for clause with all three numerals computed: `openingCreditRedemptionsCovered()` in `maanta-app/src/lib/merchant-ledger-copy.ts` floors the granted credit over the `app_config` success fee, so a fee change moves the count with no code edit and the literal 10 never enters the codebase. The credit amount is read from the merchant's own ledger row rather than `app_config`, so an older merchant's credit is not restated at today's promo value. **The second ruling was not available at ship time and was recorded as a choice, not a silence; the founder closed it on 2026-08-16 on the shipped behaviour** — see the entry in `docs/maanta-decisions-log.md`. The brief scopes the state to a "new merchant" without defining when it stops, so it renders only while the credit is unspent — no `success_fee` or `success_fee_arrears` row yet — because "your first N redemptions covered" stops being true at the first charge. There is deliberately **no partly-spent state**: showing the notice past the first success fee would state something untrue, so it needs a new ruling and new copy from the brief, not a predicate tweak. It renders last in the wallet's state chain, so any real warning wins and no merchant sees two states at once, and it uses a new neutral `info` variant of `InlineAlert` rather than rust: good news in a be-careful colour is the same colour-semantics error D80 corrected. Guard: `maanta-app/src/lib/__tests__/merchant-ledger-copy.test.ts` pins the derivation at three fee/credit combinations, the null return when no honest count exists, the unspent-only trigger, the neutral variant, the ordering behind Low balance, and — via the shared comment stripper (D38) — that none of the brief's three numerals appear in code. Verified: lint, typecheck, `npm test` (91 files, 732 tests), `npm run build` with all three post-build gates | founder |

| D106 | closed | code-outlier | 2026-08-16 | Onboarding / attribution | **Admin-assisted onboarding was unreachable in production, and the only way to reach it would have recorded a false actor.** `merchants.onboarding_mode` has carried `admin_assisted` since `20260702083812`, and `onboard_merchant` produces it — but only on the authenticated-caller branch, where `current_user_role()` is `admin`. Production has no such branch available: Clerk is the launch strategy, so a route handler has no user-scoped Postgres identity (`maanta-app/src/lib/supabase/` offers a cookie client and a service_role client, nothing carrying an admin into Postgres). Every server write goes through service_role, whose branch derived attribution from parameters alone and could only produce `self_serve` or `agent_assisted`, stamping `onboarded_by_user_id = p_user_id`. So an admin onboarding a shop would have been recorded as **the merchant self-serving** — false in the one column that says who did what, on a table dispute and fraud review read. Founder asked for the capability 2026-08-16 | **Fix written, NOT yet applied — the feature is inert until a human applies it, by design.** `maanta-app/supabase/migrations/20260816020000_admin_assisted_onboarding_attribution.sql` adds a trailing, defaulted `p_admin_user_id` honoured only on the service_role branch, validated to reference a real `admin`, mutually exclusive with agent attribution, and rejected outright on the authenticated branch where the caller is already the admin. `maanta-app/src/app/api/admin/merchants/onboard/route.ts` **fails closed with a 503 naming the migration** rather than retrying without the parameter — that retry is the false-record path, and its absence is pinned by `maanta-app/src/lib/__tests__/admin-onboard-merchant.test.ts`, which also pins that the merchant-authored route never sends admin attribution. SQL scenarios: `maanta-app/supabase/tests/admin_assisted_onboarding_test.sql` (admin actor recorded, self-serve unchanged, agent unchanged, non-admin rejected with no row written and no role promoted, both-attributions rejected). → **Closed 2026-08-16 — founder-authorized apply to production, read back.** Applied via MCP `execute_sql` rather than `apply_migration`, deliberately: `apply_migration` mints its own wall-clock ledger version, which is D86 and caused D24, so the DDL was run directly and the ledger row written separately (see **D107** — that write was blocked and is outstanding). Read-back on `axrrslqssmbngbataejg`: exactly **one** `onboard_merchant(uuid,text,text,text,text,text,text,text,text,text,uuid,uuid)` — the 11-argument overload is gone, so the ambiguity that would have broken merchant self-onboarding does not exist; `p_admin_user_id` present; `proacl` reads `postgres=X`, `authenticated=X`, `service_role=X` and nothing else — **no `anon`, no `PUBLIC`**, confirming the re-lock that CI's failure exposed; the body contains both the admin validation and the both-attributions rejection. Behaviour probed live under a `service_role` JWT claim with a non-existent user id, so both raise before any insert and wrote nothing (`ZZPROBE` row count 0): the merchant-authored route's exact 11 named arguments returned `user_not_found`, proving **binding still resolves for merchant self-onboarding** after the signature change, and the admin path with a non-admin id returned `invalid_attribution: p_admin_user_id does not reference an admin`. `/admin/merchants/new` therefore serves instead of returning its 503. Guards: `maanta-app/supabase/migrations/20260816020000_admin_assisted_onboarding_attribution.sql`, `maanta-app/supabase/tests/admin_assisted_onboarding_test.sql` (five scenarios, green in CI `db-tests`), `maanta-app/src/lib/__tests__/admin-onboard-merchant.test.ts` | founder |

| D108 | closed | code-outlier | 2026-08-16 | Auth / identity | **Changing the Clerk instance silently re-identifies every person who has ever signed in, and nothing in the repo says so.** `maanta-app/src/lib/auth.ts` resolves the app user from the Clerk JWT `sub` alone — `.eq("clerk_user_id", userId)`, and on a miss it upserts a **new** row with `role: "customer"` (`onConflict: "clerk_user_id"`). A Clerk `sub` is scoped to the instance that minted it, so after an instance change the same human arrives with a `sub` that matches no row, and one of two things happens, decided by a UNIQUE constraint rather than by any decision: (a) if `public.users.phone` is NULL for their old row, the insert succeeds and they get a **second account** — new id, `role = 'customer'`, no claims, no merchant, no admin rights, while the original row keeps a `clerk_user_id` no live instance will ever present again; or (b) if Clerk returns the same phone the old row holds, the insert violates `users_phone_key` (`UNIQUE (phone)`, read back from production), the catch path re-reads by `clerk_user_id`, finds nothing, and `ensureAppUser` returns **null** — a hard failure with no account at all. Measured on `axrrslqssmbngbataejg` 2026-08-16: **9** rows carry a `clerk_user_id`, **3 of them `admin`**; **8 of the 9 have `phone` NULL** (path a) and **1 has a phone set** (path b). Email is *not* unique and is not consulted, so there is no fallback of any kind. This is not hypothetical for MAANTA: D99 measured the development instance on 2026-08-14 and the production instance on 2026-08-16, i.e. the instance did change, across rows created 2026-07-22 to 2026-08-16, with a 3-person friends-and-family pilot as the live gate. The claim side is silence plus one near-miss: `docs/skills/clerk-auth.md:185` documents the *legacy `auth_uid`* migration case and `docs/ops/test-accounts.md:14` gives a `UPDATE ... SET clerk_user_id` relink recipe for "if a new Clerk user is created instead" — the mechanic exists in the docs as a seeding footnote, and nowhere as the operational hazard it is | **Open — needs a ruling before it needs code, and the two are different questions.** (1) *Repair*: whether the 9 existing rows need their `clerk_user_id` re-pointed to production-instance `sub`s before the pilot, which is the `docs/ops/test-accounts.md:14` statement run per person, and which nobody can run without the new `sub` in hand — an admin locked out by path (a) is the most expensive version to discover live. (2) *Prevention*: whether `ensureAppUser` should gain a verified-identity fallback (Clerk marks email/phone verification state, so "same verified email ⇒ same person" is defensible, but it is a real security decision — email match is identity only if the new instance verified it — and this register does not get to make it). Deliberately NOT fixed in this row's change: a fallback written on assumption is worse than a documented hazard, and path (b)'s hard null is at least loud. Evidence: `maanta-app/src/lib/auth.ts:61-102`, `users_phone_key` on production, **D99**'s two dated measurements. **The shape has already occurred once, independent of cause:** `admin@maanta.app` holds **two `admin` rows** with different `clerk_user_id`s (created 2026-07-22 and 2026-07-26); the newer has 25 `admin_ops_log` rows, most recent 2026-08-16 02:20 UTC, and the older has zero — one working account and one shadow. Two Clerk sign-in methods on a single instance produce that same shape, so this is not offered as proof the instance change caused it; it is proof the duplicate is reachable here, on the account whose loss is most expensive. Detection queries, the decisive sign-out/sign-in test, and the repair — which must clear the duplicate's `clerk_user_id` before writing it onto the real row, because `users_clerk_user_id_key` is UNIQUE — are in `docs/ops/clerk-instance-identity-repair.md`. → **Narrowed 2026-08-16 on evidence: the repair half is answered for the account that mattered, and no repair is needed.** The working admin row `d8c1aa1e-f89e-4aeb-8ec1-e42b06101409` (`admin@maanta.app`, Clerk id prefix `user_3H1aB…`, `phone` NULL) carries **29** `admin_ops_log` rows, **4 of them written after 14:51 UTC on 2026-08-16** — the moment D99 measured production serving the *production* Clerk instance — with the most recent at 18:53 UTC. A Clerk session is verified against the serving instance's keys, and `logAdminOp` is only reached through `requireAdminApi()` → `ensureAppUser()` → `currentClerkUserId()`, so a `sub` minted by the retired development instance could not have produced those writes: verification would have failed, `ensureAppUser` would have returned null, and the request would never have reached the audit insert. The `sub` on that row is therefore recognised by the instance serving production today, and because Clerk user ids are stable per instance per user, **a fresh sign-in returns the same id** — the sign-out/sign-in test in the runbook is moot for this account and must not be run as though it were still open. **What is NOT answered:** (1) the *prevention* half — the structural hazard is unchanged, and a future instance switch re-identifies everyone exactly as described above; (2) the other **8** Clerk-linked rows — seven customers plus the shadow admin `16cb15b5-22b5-41e7-8a9a-cc47deb3cd8f` (same email, created 2026-07-22, **zero** ops) — whose provenance is untested. Their loss is cheap: test accounts with no history, and an orphan there costs a fresh empty account rather than console access. The shadow row is inert and should be left alone; deleting it touches foreign keys on `redemptions`, `merchants` and `admin_ops_log`. One row would fail differently if it ever mattered: the seeded `b0000000-0000-4000-a000-000000000001` (`aragagency@gmail.com`) is the single admin with a `phone` set, so it takes the `users_phone_key` path — no duplicate row, no account, and nothing to harvest a `sub` from — but it is an `is_demo` row with zero ops. → **Prevention approach drafted 2026-08-18**: `docs/ops/clerk-instance-identity-prevention.md` — phased (Phase 1 phone-only runtime fallback, safe on today's schema because D126 made `users.phone` verified-and-immutable; Phase 2 email fallback gated on locking `users.email` + verified-only storage + exactly-one-match). Awaiting a founder ruling on the four decisions listed there. No code changed by the draft. → **T4 sweep 2026-08-19:** **mechanism unchanged; its measured distribution is now moving, and toward the worse branch.** `maanta-app/src/lib/auth.ts` still resolves the app user from the Clerk `sub` alone and still upserts a new `customer` row on a miss, so both paths this row describes are intact. What changed today is which path a given person would take: **D129** added a NULL-only backfill of `public.users.phone` on sign-in, so a user who verifies a phone in Clerk now gets that column populated. **Two corrections to how that was first written, both from a live re-measurement on 2026-08-19.** First, **the decay has not started**: `public.users` now carries **10** Clerk-linked rows, **9 on path (a)** and **1 on path (b)** — and that one is the seeded demo admin, which has always had a phone. All **9 real** Clerk-linked users are still path (a); 3 of the 10 are `admin`. D129 is committed on `claude/work-in-progress-7nudnu` and is **not live**: production still serves `main @ 5040acda`, and the branch's deployments are previews carrying `target: null`, so no promote occurred. The migration begins at the first sign-in with a verified Clerk phone *after* D129 deploys, not before. Second, **"worse" needs splitting into two axes, because they point opposite ways.** Path (b) is *louder* — a `users_phone_key` violation fails visibly and `docs/skills/prod-auth-deals-recovery.md` reasonably calls it easier to diagnose. Path (b) is *harder to repair*: path (a) leaves a duplicate row from which the new `sub` can be harvested, whereas path (b) never inserted, so repair needs the `sub` fetched from the Clerk dashboard per person. And to a shopper standing at a counter, "no account at all" is worse than "signed in but empty". At 9 users that repair is a tolerable afternoon; at 200 pilot shoppers it is not. **So this row now has a deadline it did not have yesterday, and the window is open.** D129 fixes a *certain* failure — a pre-invited staff seat will not link, guaranteed — and worsens a *conditional* one that fires only on a Clerk instance change nobody is planning, so on expected value deploying wins. But nothing is urgent in either direction: zero real staff seats exist, and no user has been created since 2026-08-16. That makes the sequencing free rather than a trade-off: **settle the prevention half below before D129 reaches production, and ship them together.** Re-measure the split immediately before that deploy. → **Ruled and implemented 2026-08-19 (founder ruling A, decision-queue Q1); the row stays open until the code is live.** The prevention half: `ensureAppUserFromClerk` (`maanta-app/src/lib/auth.ts`) now falls back, on a `sub` miss, to matching the caller's **Clerk-verified** primary email (lowercased; matched with `.eq`, never `ilike`, whose `_` wildcard could equate different mailboxes) against **real rows only** (`is_demo = false` — a person must never be identity-linked into a synthetic seed row). **Exactly one** match relinks: the row's `clerk_user_id` is overwritten with the new sub — overwriting is the repair, since after an instance change every stranded row holds a dead sub — and the request re-enters the normal path under a one-shot flag so the D129 phone backfill runs in the same sign-in. Zero matches insert a fresh account as before. **More than one match is a hard failure by ruling**: null, loud, never a guess — a wrong link hands someone another person's claims, wallet access or role. The insert path now stores only the verified email (`verifiedPrimaryEmail`), D126's rule applied to the column the moment it became load-bearing. **Measured residual, named so the hard-fail case is known to be real rather than theoretical**: production carries three duplicate-email groups — `admin@maanta.app` on **two real admin rows** (almost certainly this row's own path (a) having already fired once on the D99 instance change), the founder's address on one real admin plus one demo customer (the demo filter resolves this one), and one customer address on two real rows. **The two all-real groups will hard-fail the fallback after any future instance change until an admin resolves the duplicates** — a data decision (retire or merge one row of each pair) that a session must not take. Guards: `maanta-app/src/lib/__tests__/clerk-email-relink.test.ts` (every branch: relink, re-entry, one-shot recursion bound, ambiguity hard-fail with PII-free logging, unverified skip, race recovery — **proved non-vacuous by deleting the fallback and watching 8 tests fail**) and the instance-change end-to-end case in `maanta-app/src/lib/__tests__/staff-seat-linking-chain.test.ts` (dead sub + verified email + verified phone → the one original row relinked, no duplicate inserted, phone backfilled and the staff seat linked in the same sign-in). Companion hardening: **D142** freezes `users.email` in the D124 trigger, because the fallback makes it an access-control input. Close this row when production serves the fallback (merged through `main`, never a promote) — the repair half then self-serves on each person's next sign-in → **Closed 2026-08-19: production serves the fallback, and the freeze behind it is live.** PR #235 merged through `main` as squash `693ca47` on a fully green CI run — never a promote — and the production deployment went READY at 22:43 UTC: `target: production`, ref `main`, the merge SHA, aliased to the apex and www domains, with the served manifest output confirming the new tree behaviorally (the D37 lesson — verify trees and behavior, not SHAs). The companion freeze **D142** was applied and read back the same hour, so the manufactured-ambiguity window the fallback opened is shut. The repair half now self-serves: each stranded person's next sign-in relinks their own row, phone backfill and staff-seat link included. Residual, unchanged and founder-owned: the two all-real duplicate-email groups will hard-fail the single-match rule after any future instance change until one row of each pair is retired or merged — a data decision this register does not take. Guards: `maanta-app/src/lib/__tests__/clerk-email-relink.test.ts` (every branch, proved non-vacuous by deletion) and the instance-change end-to-end case in `maanta-app/src/lib/__tests__/staff-seat-linking-chain.test.ts` → **Residual resolved 2026-08-20 (founder-instructed data operation: "resolve the duplicate email pairs").** Measured first, across all eleven FK paths into `public.users`: the shadow admin row `16cb15b5-22b5-41e7-8a9a-cc47deb3cd8f` carried zero `admin_ops_log` rows (one inert `agents` seed reference — the FK that already forbade deletion) against the keeper's 29; **both** rows of the customer pair carried zero activity everywhere, so the keeper is the Clerk-provenance row `bf197f6e-4d94-4bcb-bf15-fe4511371080` (named, matching the live auth strategy) and the loser the Supabase-era row `2373fca4-955b-474d-a4c1-68f9a205ce94` (no Clerk sub ever, `auth_uid` left intact). Resolution: `email` set NULL on the two loser rows only — no delete, no role change, no other column — via the service_role claim the D142 trigger admits, each UPDATE guarded on the expected prior email so a concurrent change would no-op. Read back on `axrrslqssmbngbataejg`: both keepers are now the sole real carriers of their addresses and **zero email addresses are carried by more than one real row**; the one surviving duplicate group is the founder's address on one real admin plus one demo customer, already resolved by the fallback's `is_demo` filter and left untouched by the never-touch-demo-data rule. The relink is now deterministic for every address in production. no guard beyond the existing relink suite: a data state, not a code path — re-run the duplicate-groups census before any future Clerk instance work  | founder |

The original three keep their keys AND their labels, so no stored row changes meaning. The four orphans are filed where a shopper would look: screen protector and earbuds under `electronics`, prayer mat under `home`, suitcase under `shoes` — nothing is mis-filed and the demo catalogue now has **zero** uncategorised items, spanning nine of the ten buckets (nothing at BBS Mall is a Health & pharmacy deal, and inventing one would put a shop on the demo feed that does not exist). Because `20260818150000` had never been applied (**D116**), the widening is an edit to that migration rather than a follow-up — the CHECK lists ten keys from the start. **If that migration is ever found to have been applied while it still listed three keys, editing it is not enough:** the ledger will treat it as done, `db push` will skip it, and production will refuse seven of the ten keys with a `check_violation`; widen the live constraint by hand and record it. The migration and `docs/ops/supabase-migrations.md` both say so. Negative cases now name `__not_a_category__`, which can never be promoted | founder |

Below those counts the result is exact. The rail (deal-type) filter has never had this problem because each rail is its own query.
