The shopper is not left stranded: `claim_deal` still raises `deal_paused` and the API returns 409, so no claim succeeds. What is wrong is that a merchant who paused a deal is still advertising it, and the shopper's dead end arrives at the tap rather than never being offered. Found while adding category filtering, which audited every surface reading deal rows | **One predicate — add `.eq("is_paused", false)` to both selects in `maanta-app/src/app/(shopper)/search/page.tsx`** (the title/description query and the by-shop query), matching `selectLiveDealBucket` in `maanta-app/src/lib/data.ts`. | D120 | closed | code-outlier | 2026-08-18 | Shopper / discovery + merchant edit | **Three defects the category change shipped into review, all found by an adversarial pass over its own diff, and all of the same species: a surface reporting something that was not true.** (1) **The merchant edit sheet reported a category correction as saved when it had been discarded.** `PATCH /api/deals/[id]` dropped `category` on a missing-column error, applied the rest, and returned a bare `{ok: true}` — byte-identical to full success. The client branches only on `res.ok`, so it closed the sheet on the success affordance; `router.refresh()` does not remount the component, so its local state kept the chip selected and the loss never surfaced on re-open. This was live-relevant immediately: production has no `category` column (**D116**), and that sheet is the documented correction path for pre-taxonomy deals — i.e. every deal that exists. (2) **The feed told shoppers the mall was empty when it was not.** `feedEmptyState`'s predecessor decided between "quiet mall" and "your category" by asking whether any category chips were on offer — but the chip row is deliberately withheld when every live deal sits in one bucket, so a node with five live `fashion` deals and a `?category=food` URL rendered "No deals live right now". It also never considered the deal-type filter, which can empty the screen while the copy blames the category. `/browse` had the same hole: `browseEmptyState` never received the category, and on the favourites chip asserted "your saved merchants have no live deals in this node" when a category filter was what removed them. (3) **Nothing guarded the feature itself** — deleting `filterDealsByCategory` from the browse surface left the whole suite green, because every guard checked a taxonomy, a URL parser, a pure helper or a string in a source file, and none rendered a deal list | **Closed 2026-08-18, in the same PR that opened it.** (1) The route now returns `categorySaved: false` when it applied an edit without the category, the sheet stays open and says so, and an edit that was *only* a category it could not store returns **503 with the deal unchanged** rather than bumping `updated_at` and answering ok; an unrecognised key no longer reports as "Nothing to update". (2) `maanta-app/src/lib/feed-empty-state.ts` derives the copy from the deal counts at each filtering stage and names the filter that actually emptied the screen — extracted into its own module precisely so it can be tested as behaviour; `browseEmptyState` takes the same input. `DealCategoryChips` now renders a lone **All** chip when a category is active with no options behind it, so the filter is always clearable. (3) Guard: `maanta-app/src/components/__tests__/category-filtering.test.ts` renders the real component with real rows — **verified to bite by deleting the filter, which fails 4 of its 6 cases** — plus behavioural cases for `feedEmptyState` in `maanta-app/src/lib/__tests__/deal-categories.test.ts`. Two smaller corrections rode along: `selectDealsWithMerchants` now throws the **last** error rather than the first (an earlier one is by construction a missing column it chose to work around, so surfacing it points the operator at the wrong problem — this reverses an earlier decision in the same PR and makes all three degradation helpers agree), and the demo-catalogue guard counts keys against catalogue **items** instead of a literal 16. The lesson worth keeping: a guard that greps source for a conditional cannot catch a conditional that is present and wrong — which is exactly how (2) passed its own test | eng |

(2) **Resolve the D115–D119 row-ID collision** before the second merge, by renumbering the later branch's rows and fixing their cross-references. **Closed 2026-08-19 by merging `claude/security-audit-maanta-peeazf`.** The repo now contains all five, and a full read-back diff of `supabase_migrations.schema_migrations` against `ls supabase/migrations/` shows **96 rows and 96 files, identical on every version/name pair** — the first complete reconciliation since the 87/87 of 2026-08-08. The row-ID half was resolved in the same merge: the security branch's D115–D119 became **D123–D127**, because `main` had issued those numbers first and the register is append-only. | D122 | closed | code-outlier | 2026-08-18 | Shopper / discovery | **Category filtering is live on production and no shopper can see it, because every deal that exists predates the column.** Measured immediately after the **D116** apply: `deals_public_browse` returns **260** live discoverable deals and **0** of them carry a category. `dealCategoryChips` deliberately returns `[]` when no live deal has one, so the chip row does not render and `/feed` and `/browse` look exactly as they did before the migration. Zero risk, zero work, unpredictable timing. (b) **Force one reseed cycle** by retiring the current demo flash deals (`UPDATE public.deals SET is_active = FALSE WHERE is_demo AND deal_type = 'flash'`) and calling `public.reseed_demo_flash_deals()`, which then creates up to 40 categorised ones. Demo-scoped and reversible, but it churns the rehearsal feed. (c) **Back-fill the existing demo deals' categories from their titles.** Fastest and most complete, and legitimate ONLY because these are `is_demo` fixture rows — the same title-guessing is explicitly forbidden for real merchant deals (see `docs/skills/deal-categories.md`), and the two must not be confused. Deliberately not done unprompted: it writes invented categorisation to ~260 production rows. Reversible with `UPDATE public.deals SET category = NULL WHERE is_demo`. **Closed 2026-08-19 by option (a) — it resolved itself overnight, no intervention.** The autoreseed cycled the demo flash deals as they expired and re-created them from the categorised catalogue. Re-measured ~10h after the apply: `deals_public_browse` returns **268** live discoverable deals, **35** of which carry a category across **9 of the 10** buckets — food 8, shoes 7, beauty 4, fashion 4, jewellery 3, kids 3, electronics 2, home 2, services 2, and 233 still uncategorised (the fixed seed batches, which the autoreseed does not touch). Only `health` is empty, which is the deliberate gap recorded in `20260818160000`. `dealCategoryChips` needs two or more buckets to render, so the chip row will appear. Neither of the two riskier options — forcing a reseed cycle, or back-filling ~260 rows with title-guessed categories — was needed, which is the argument for having waited: the cheapest option was also the correct one, and it wrote nothing. **What remains is deployment, not data.** Production still serves `main`, which has no category code — PR #233 is unmerged, and every deployment of it so far is a Vercel Preview. The chip row appears on the live site the moment that PR merges and deploys, with no further database work. **Whichever way the rehearsal data goes, a real merchant publishing through the wizard is categorised from their first deal — the wizard requires it**, which `maanta-app/src/lib/__tests__/deal-categories.test.ts` pins (Continue is disabled without a category and `/api/deals` returns 400). no guard: no test in this repo can observe production's live deal counts or the autoreseed's output — the control is the re-measurement recorded above, re-run before the claim is repeated | founder |
| D123 | closed | code-outlier | 2026-08-17 | Security / RLS | **The write lockdown on `public.merchants` is claimed and tested, and it is bypassable through `merchants_public_browse` by any signed-in user.** `maanta-app/supabase/migrations/20260723120000_revoke_authenticated_writes_core_tables.sql` states the posture — "a stolen merchant JWT could PATCH privileged columns directly via PostgREST … C-1 merchants — tier, account_balance, trust_metric, status, is_shadow_banned" — and `maanta-app/supabase/tests/revoke_authenticated_writes_core_tables_test.sql` Scenario B asserts the UPDATE is refused. Both are true **of the table**. Neither covers the view. One day after that revoke, `20260723130000_fix_browse_views_security_invoker.sql` set `security_invoker = false` on the two browse views — deliberately and correctly, since anon's SELECT on the base tables had just been revoked and an invoker view could not serve pre-sign-in browse. The combination is the defect, and no single migration contains it: (1) `merchants_public_browse` selects from one table with no join and only plain column references, so PostgreSQL makes it **auto-updatable** — read back from production, `information_schema.views.is_updatable = YES` and all 14 columns `is_updatable = YES`; (2) `security_invoker = false` performs the underlying write as the view **owner**, `postgres`, which holds `rolbypassrls`, so RLS on `merchants` is not applied at all; (3) the REVOKE named the TABLE, so the view kept Supabase's default `INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE` grant to `authenticated`, which nothing has ever revoked. Measured on `axrrslqssmbngbataejg` 2026-08-17: under `SET LOCAL ROLE authenticated`, `EXPLAIN UPDATE public.merchants_public_browse SET tier='elite', is_featured=true WHERE node='BBS Mall'` plans as **`Update on merchants … rows=40`** with only the view's own WHERE as a filter and no RLS qual; the identical statement against `public.merchants` raises `42501 permission denied for table merchants`. The caller needs no merchant account — **any shopper with a session** and the publishable anon key qualifies. Reachable columns are the view's list: `tier` (free Elite, KES 3,500/mo), `status`, `is_visible`, `is_featured`, `trust_metric` (a fraud input), and the physical-location fields a shopper walks to — `what3words_address`, `floor`, `unit_number`. The view's WHERE limits which rows are reachable (active, visible, non-shadow-banned) but not which of them a statement may touch, so every other live merchant at the node is in scope: this is competitor sabotage as much as self-promotion. `account_balance` is not a view column, so the wallet was never directly writable, and `deals_public_browse` is not auto-updatable (it joins `merchants`) so its identical grant is inert **today** — inert by accident of shape, which is the same accident that produced this row | **Fix written, NOT applied — a human must apply it.** `maanta-app/supabase/migrations/20260817120000_revoke_authenticated_writes_browse_views.sql` revokes `INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE, TRUNCATE` from `anon, authenticated` on all four `public` views, not just the exploitable one; reads and `security_invoker = false` are untouched, because the flag is what makes anon browse work and is not the defect. Verified as a **rolled-back rehearsal** on production 2026-08-17 (`BEGIN; REVOKE …; asserts; ROLLBACK`): the guard query goes clean, anon still reads 211 merchant rows, and the tier-escalation UPDATE raises `insufficient_privilege`; the post-rollback re-count confirms production still carries all 12 offending grants, i.e. nothing was applied. Guards: `maanta-app/supabase/tests/browse_views_test.sql` Scenario E (grant posture), F (behavioural — the exact escalation is refused and the base row is unchanged) and **G, the ratchet** — a catalog query that fails on *any* view in `public` granting a write to anon/authenticated, dry-run against production where it correctly names all 12. G is the guard that would have caught this: whether a view is exploitable depends on auto-updatability and `security_invoker`, two properties that flipped silently across three unrelated migrations (`20260723130000`, `20260726120000`, `20260729141000`), whereas the grant is always load-bearing. Close on read-back showing zero offending grants on production. Apply note: **D86** applies — an MCP `apply_migration` mints its own ledger version, and **D107** already has the ledger one row out; prefer `supabase db push`, or record the filename version by hand. → **Closed 2026-08-17 (founder-authorized MCP apply).** Applied to production and verified by read-back: the Scenario-G catalog query returns **0** offending view grants, and anon retains SELECT on `merchants_public_browse` and `deals_public_browse`. Ledger row `20260817120000` recorded with the filename version | founder |
| D124 | closed | code-outlier | 2026-08-17 | Auth / access-control | **Any signed-in shopper can freeze-frame their own identity: `public.users.phone` (and `clerk_user_id`, `auth_uid`) are self-writable via PostgREST, and a written phone is the primitive behind a merchant_staff-seat hijack.** RLS `users_own_row` is `FOR ALL USING (id = current_user_id())` with no WITH CHECK, and `authenticated` holds the default UPDATE grant on `users`; the only column protected was `role`, by the `prevent_self_role_escalation` trigger (`20260705200856`). `phone`/`clerk_user_id`/`auth_uid` are identity, not profile: `current_user_id()`/`current_user_role()` resolve the caller from `clerk_user_id = auth.jwt()->>'sub' OR auth_uid::text = sub`, and `getMerchantContext` (`maanta-app/src/lib/merchant.ts:53-70`) links a signed-in user into a **pre-invited** `merchant_staff` seat whenever `users.phone` equals a `merchant_staff.phone` whose `user_id` is still NULL — and on match promotes the user's role to `merchant_staff` and hands them that seat's `can_verify`/`can_deals`/`can_topup`/`can_purchase`. So a shopper who knows a shop's pre-registered, not-yet-signed-in staff phone can PATCH their own `users.phone` to it (the target phone is free of the `users_phone_key` UNIQUE until that staff member first signs in) and, on the next merchant request, be linked as that staff member — able to verify redemptions (charging the merchant KES 30 each) and spend the merchant's wallet on boosts, at a shop they have no relationship with. `users.phone` is assumed to be the Clerk-verified number written once at provisioning (`maanta-app/src/lib/auth.ts`); the row's holder overwriting it with an arbitrary unverified value breaks that assumption at the one place staff identity keys on it. Proven read-only on production 2026-08-17: under `SET LOCAL ROLE authenticated` with a crafted JWT, `UPDATE public.users SET phone=… WHERE id=<self>` updated 1 row; the same against another id updated 0 (RLS); a direct `role` change still raised. The direct-write of `merchant_staff` is NOT the vector — `staff_owner_manage`'s WITH CHECK confines inserts to merchants the caller owns; the phone column is | **Fix written, NOT applied.** `maanta-app/supabase/migrations/20260817130000_prevent_users_identity_self_change.sql` adds a BEFORE UPDATE trigger mirroring `prevent_self_role_escalation`: if `phone`/`clerk_user_id`/`auth_uid` change, require service_role or admin. Column-scoped, so the legitimate writes survive — `push_subscription` (the one column the anon/authenticated client writes, `maanta-app/src/app/api/push/subscribe/route.ts`), and everything the service client does (provisioning, the profile route, the staff role-bump) which runs as service_role. Verified every `users` write in `src/` is either service-role or touches only `push_subscription`. Guard: `maanta-app/supabase/tests/users_identity_immutable_test.sql` — Scenario A (identity columns frozen, push_subscription still writable), B (the staff-seat hijack blocked end to end), C (service_role and admin writes unaffected). **Not yet live — apply via `supabase db push`** per `docs/ops/supabase-migrations.md` (D86/D107 apply caveat). Close on read-back: the trigger present on `public.users` and a `SET LOCAL ROLE authenticated` phone-write raising. → **Closed 2026-08-17 (founder-authorized MCP apply).** Applied and verified by read-back: `prevent_identity_self_change_trigger` is present on `public.users`; a live `SET LOCAL ROLE authenticated` phone-write raises `unauthorized` while a `push_subscription` self-write still succeeds (rolled-back probe). Ledger row `20260817130000` recorded | eng |

| D127 | closed | code-outlier | 2026-08-18 | Merchant / staff | **The staff invite promises "Permissions apply from first sign-in", but the server stored the phone raw and linked seats by exact string, so any non-canonical spelling silently never links.** `getMerchantContext` links a pre-invited `merchant_staff` seat by matching `merchant_staff.phone` against the Clerk-provisioned `public.users.phone` (E.164, e.g. `+254712345678`) with `.eq()` — exact string equality. `maanta-app/src/app/api/staff/route.ts` stored `String(phone).trim()` verbatim: no normalisation, no validation beyond presence. The convention is not | **Normalise every `frontend` entry to a single `maanta-app/`-relative form, then simplify the guard to a plain existence check.** Deliberately not done here: rewriting twenty-one entries unrelated to this session's change would bury two real corrections in a large diff, and the entries are correct-but-ambiguous rather than wrong. In the meantime `maanta-app/src/lib/__tests__/frames-inventory.test.ts` resolves each entry through the three forms and then a unique-tail search, and fails when a citation matches nothing or is ambiguous — which is what caught both of the above. Worth pairing with **D26**, since a guardrail about what `design-ahead` frames must cite is easier to write once every citation resolves. → **Closed 2026-08-19**, the same day, by doing the normalisation this row deferred: all **20** unrooted `frontend` entries rewritten to the single `maanta-app/`-relative convention (each resolved mechanically through the same three-form-plus-unique-tail search the interim guard used, so no entry was retargeted by hand), and the resolver in `maanta-app/src/lib/__tests__/frames-inventory.test.ts` **deleted in the same change**, replaced by a plain `existsSync` check — a lenient matcher kept beyond its purpose teaches the next author that any spelling is fine, which is how the three conventions accumulated in the first place. **Proved non-vacuous**: reintroducing a retired-route-group citation turns the guard red. Guard: `maanta-app/src/lib/__tests__/frames-inventory.test.ts` (`cites only frontend paths that exist`), now exact rather than a search | eng |
| D141 | closed | code-outlier | 2026-08-19 | PWA install | **The manifest `start_url` is the one URL an installed user cannot navigate away from, and nothing asserts it points anywhere.** `maanta-app/src/app/manifest.ts` sets `start_url: "/app-bootstrap"`. An installed PWA opens there in a standalone window with no address bar, so a shopper who lands on a broken bootstrap has no route back — and `/app-bootstrap` has no UI of its own, it only resolves a role and redirects, so a failure presents as a blank or stuck screen rather than an error anyone would report. `maanta-app/src/lib/pwa/__tests__/app-bootstrap.test.ts` exists and does **not** cover this: it asserts `destinationForRole` for six roles plus the unknown fallback, and separately checks `/download` landing copy. Nothing connects the manifest to the route. So the contract can break in three ways no test would notice — the `start_url` string could be edited to a path that does not exist, the route directory could be renamed or deleted, or the redirect could stop resolving for a signed-out visitor, which is what an install prompt's audience mostly is | **Propose, do not build blind — the shape depends on what the contract is taken to be.** Narrowest and cheapest: assert in a test that the manifest's `start_url` resolves to a page file that exists, which catches the rename and the typo and is the same class of check `maanta-app/src/lib/__tests__/frames-inventory.test.ts` now performs for the design inventory. Wider: assert the signed-out behaviour of `/app-bootstrap` too, since an installed user who has been signed out lands there first — that needs a route-level render, which this repo does only for marketing routes today. Widest and outside a Claude session: cover it in the Playwright golden path (`docs/ops/e2e-golden-path.md`), which is the only place a real navigation is exercised. Related: **D139**, which records that no one has yet walked this landing on a device at all, so the route's real-world behaviour is unobserved as well as unguarded. → **Closed 2026-08-19**, at the narrowest shape this row proposes plus the two route classes adjacent to it. `maanta-app/src/lib/pwa/__tests__/app-bootstrap.test.ts` now maps app-router URLs to page files by walking `src/app` with route groups and optional catch-alls elided — the same resolution Next performs — and asserts three things an installed user is sent to with no address bar to correct: the manifest `start_url` resolves to a real page, the signed-out bounce target `/login` exists, and **every** `destinationForRole` target exists (all six roles plus the fallback). **Proved non-vacuous**: a one-character typo in `start_url` turns the suite red and names the broken URL. The wider shapes stay deliberately unbuilt — signed-out behaviour needs a route-level render this repo does only for marketing pages, and the real navigation belongs in the Playwright golden path; both are recorded in the guard's own docblock so the limit is stated where the next reader will look. The device measurement remains **D139** and is untouched by this closure | eng |

So the PRD-level claim that the ledger is the financial source of truth holds for `charged` and `owed` and is structurally false for `unknown` — before **and after** resolution. Do not implement ahead of the ruling; the frozen `{charged, owed, unknown}` model and verify-anyway are untouched by this row | founder |

| D147 | closed | code-outlier | 2026-08-20 | Security / RLS | **The READ twin of D123: any signed-in shopper can read every active merchant's wallet balance, arrears and contact PII straight off `public.merchants`.** D123 closed the WRITE path through `merchants_public_browse`; the READ path into the base tables underneath was never examined. `20260723120000_revoke_authenticated_writes_core_tables.sql` **kept** `GRANT SELECT ON public.merchants TO authenticated` ("if ever needed; the app reads via service_role"), and the baseline's `merchants_customer_read` policy is `FOR SELECT USING (status='active' AND is_visible AND NOT is_shadow_banned)` — roles=public, PERMISSIVE, so it applies to `authenticated`. RLS is row-level and cannot restrict columns, so a policy meant to expose the storefront exposes ALL 38 columns of every active merchant — including `account_balance`, `outstanding_arrears`, `phone`, `email`, `whatsapp`, `trust_metric`. Proven on production 2026-08-20: a `SET LOCAL ROLE authenticated` session for a shopper with no merchant read another merchant's `account_balance` (`777.00`), arrears, phone and email from `public.merchants`; `redemptions` and `merchant_transactions` returned 0 rows (their policies are user-scoped), localising the defect to the two projection tables that own a `*_public_browse` view. `anon` was spared only by accident — evaluating the merchants policy set also runs `merchants_own`/`merchants_agent_read`, which call `current_user_id()`, and `anon` lacks EXECUTE on it, so its base read raised 42501 instead of leaking. That accident is why `browse_views_test.sql` Scenario B is green for the wrong reason: it asserts anon cannot read the base table and gets `insufficient_privilege` from the function grant, while never testing `authenticated`, where the leak was. `deals` carries the identical `deals_customer_read` + grant (lower-sensitivity columns, same class) | **Closed 2026-08-20 (founder-authorized apply).** `maanta-app/supabase/migrations/20260820120000_revoke_customer_base_table_reads_merchants_deals.sql` revokes base-table SELECT from `anon, authenticated` on `merchants` and `deals` and drops the two `*_customer_read` policies; reads go through the `security_invoker=false` `*_public_browse` views, which run as owner and need no base grant. Applied to production and verified by read-back: `has_table_privilege` SELECT is **false** for anon and authenticated on both base tables, both customer_read policies are **gone**, the browse views still serve 211 rows, ledger row `20260820120000` recorded at the filename version — production at 98 rows. This is the one migration production held that `main` still lacked after the security-branch merges (D121); landing the file brings the repo to 98, matching prod. Guards: `maanta-app/supabase/tests/browse_views_test.sql` Scenario **H** (base tables grant no anon/authenticated SELECT), **I** (behavioural — `authenticated`, the role Scenario B missed, is refused the balance/PII columns while the view row stays visible) and **J**, the read-side ratchet — any base table feeding a `*_public_browse` view must not grant base SELECT to anon/authenticated, generalised via `view_table_usage`. A future RLS-governed authenticated read must use a COLUMN-scoped grant + scoped policy, never a table-wide grant with a broad policy. "Phone re-enabled" is **removed** from this condition. **Progress 2026-08-22, late evening (closing-pass doc §3a): admin half PROVEN** — `admin@maanta.app` signed in by email code and resolved to the existing `d8c1aa1e…` row with no new row; **merchant and shopper still unmatched** ("Couldn't find your account") because those Clerk users were phone-first and now carry no identifier. **2026-08-23, early: the two "existing" Clerk users turned out not to exist on the production instance** — a dashboard read showed exactly two users (`admin@maanta.app`, and `skattyspam@gmail.com` = `user_3HyW…`); `user_3I0X…` and `user_3GqR…` returned "No users found" by id, so the ids `public.users` carried for the merchant and shopper pointed at the development instance or deleted users (a D108 hazard instance). **Founder chose Option 1:** create the two Clerk users in the dashboard (founder did — `user_3II1XPqrtCjkwk3FYIm1a44QZxQ` for `aragagency@gmail.com`, `user_3II1e5ofFg8q9zM3rmASIz738uV` for `moe_elmi97@hotmail.co.uk`, each email primary), then relink the **existing** `public.users` rows to them so identities and history are preserved. the SQL suite gained scenarios G-J (historical rows stay NULL and are excluded from the count, the 7-day boundary includes 6d23h and excludes 8d, the index exists on the right column, and the tracking start is recorded); the shopper ticket lookup gained a deterministic `id DESC` tiebreak because `redeemed_at` is a semantically fragile recency key; and both dashboards now distinguish a real zero from a failed read from incomplete history via `maanta-app/src/lib/claims-window.ts`, which labels the card from `app_config.claims_tracking_started_at` — seeded by the migration in the same transaction that starts tracking — so a small first-week number cannot read as low demand. `maanta-app/e2e/dashboards.spec.ts` now asserts both dashboards render KPIs and numbers with no read-failure state, closing the hole that let a dead `/founder` pass on HTTP 200 plus a heading. migration `maanta-app/supabase/migrations/20260824130000_redemptions_claimed_at.sql` adds a nullable `claimed_at timestamptz`, in two statements on purpose — `ADD COLUMN` with **no** default so Postgres does not backfill historical rows with the migration timestamp, then `SET DEFAULT now()` so every future insert is stamped by the database and never by a caller. `claim_deal` is deliberately **not** modified: the column default already guarantees the stamp on every insert path, and re-issuing that function would put the D25 pause gate and the CSPRNG OTP draw back in play for no gain. Both dashboards repointed to `claimed_at`, and `/admin` gained the honest read-failure guard it lacked, reusing the existing `LeadsReadError` component. **A THIRD consumer of the phantom column was found by a parallel trace, and it is shopper-facing:** `maanta-app/src/app/(shopper)/deals/[id]/page.tsx` ordered its existing-ticket lookup by `created_at`, so the query errored, `existingTicketId` came back null, and the page offered "Claim deal" to a shopper who already held a live pending ticket — the 409 the earlier E2E recorded as a correct refusal was the API backstop covering a broken screen. Fixed to `.order("redeemed_at")`, deliberately not the new `claimed_at`, whose NULLs sort first under DESC and would surface a stale pre-migration ticket. Guards: `maanta-app/supabase/tests/redemptions_claimed_at_test.sql` (column shape, server-stamped at claim, unchanged by successful verification, unchanged by failed verification, refused re-claim creates no second row, and the KPI's own filter finds the claim) and `maanta-app/src/lib/__tests__/claims-metric.test.ts` (12 source ratchets, verified to fail 3/12 against the old code). **Historical data: nothing was backfilled.** Every redemption created before this migration has `claimed_at IS NULL`, because a claim time that was never recorded is unknowable and inventing one would put fabricated data on a money-adjacent audit record. Claims (7d) is therefore authoritative only from the migration forward, which the column COMMENT states. Superseded proposal: (1) migration adding `redemptions.created_at timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now()` — new rows are correct immediately; historical rows take the migration timestamp, which is wrong for them, so consider nullable-with-no-backfill if honest history matters more than a filled column; (2) point both KPI queries at it; (3) render an honest read-failure state instead of `?? 0`, the pattern D149 already established on the founder screen, so a broken count can never again look like a real zero. **Closed 2026-08-25 — fixed, applied and deployed.** Merged as `061c92c` (PR #272, superseding the stale #264) on a full green board: `ci`, `db-tests` on real Supabase, Cursor Security Agent with no findings. Migration `20260824130000_redemptions_claimed_at.sql` applied to production under founder authorization **before** the merge — additive, and no deployed code read `claimed_at`, so applying first was a behavioural no-op that removed the window in which both consoles would be down. It was renumbered from `20260823140000` first: D162's `20260824120000` had been applied on 2026-08-24 while the PR sat open, which would have left it applying behind two already-applied versions (D121 again — read the ledger, not the directory). The apply minted `20260825083646` — **ten for ten** — and was repaired to the repo filename before any other check. Read back: ledger **102/102 by version AND name** via a full diff against `supabase/migrations/`; `claimed_at` nullable with `DEFAULT now()`; `idx_redemptions_claimed_at` present; `claims_tracking_started_at` recorded; **401 redemptions, 0 non-NULL `claimed_at`** — no history was fabricated; `claim_deal` never mentions the column and its D25 `deal_paused` gate is still live. The KPI query both dashboards run now succeeds and returns an honest 0, labelled as a partial window by `claimsWindow()` rather than presented as a confident zero — the actual defect. A second defect was found and fixed in the same change: `/admin` scanned its whole `Promise.all` result for errors, including the `app_config` claims-tracking read, directly contradicting its own comment and diverging from `/founder`; the two sets are now separated structurally. Guards: `maanta-app/src/lib/__tests__/claims-metric.test.ts` (verified to fail against the pre-fix source, on its merits — the balanced-bracket capture resolved to a real array before the exclusion assertion failed) and `maanta-app/supabase/tests/redemptions_claimed_at_test.sql`, whose scenarios A-J all passed in CI. **Not verified:** no browser check of the rendered dashboards — the sandbox proxy denies `maanta.app` | founder |

Ask the shop owner to enable it in Staff", `/merchant/topup` the equivalent for top-ups, and `/merchant/staff` itself says "Only the shop owner can manage staff". `/merchant/staff/new` renders the working form instead. **Not a security hole — the boundary holds:** `POST /api/staff` returns **403 "Only the shop owner can manage staff."** for a non-owner, verified live 2026-08-23 with a real probe. | D168 | open | code-outlier | 2026-08-24 | Security / RLS | **Four tenant-isolation RLS policies no longer filter — they raise `42501 permission denied for table merchants` instead.** `transactions_merchant` (merchant_transactions), `staff_owner_manage` (merchant_staff), `redemptions_merchant` (redemptions) and `pending_topups_merchant_read` (pending_topups) all key on `merchant_id IN (SELECT id FROM merchants WHERE user_id = current_user_id())`. A policy expression is evaluated with the **querying role's** privileges, and D147 (`20260820120000`) revoked `authenticated`'s SELECT on `public.merchants` — correctly, to close a real cross-tenant wallet/PII leak. The collateral is that these four policies, which live on *other* tables, can no longer read the table they reference. Measured on `axrrslqssmbngbataejg` 2026-08-24 by impersonating a real merchant owner (`SET LOCAL ROLE authenticated` + that owner's Clerk `sub`): a bare `select count(*) from public.merchant_transactions` raises 42501, naming `merchants`; same for `redemptions` and `merchant_staff`. Reproduced identically on a fresh DB built from all 100 migrations. **The direction of failure is closed — nothing is exposed** — and no live surface is broken, because every server route reads these tables through `createServiceClient()` (RLS-exempt) or a SECURITY DEFINER RPC. Two things make it worth a row anyway: (1) the tenant-isolation layer on four money/PII tables is inert, so isolation there currently rests on the app's service-role habit plus the *absence* of a grant, not on the policy anyone reading the schema would believe is enforcing it; (2) the obvious repair for the error — re-granting table-wide SELECT on `merchants` — reintroduces D147's CRITICAL exactly. Verified both halves in a rolled-back transaction on the fresh DB: with SELECT re-granted the four policies filter *correctly* (owner A saw 1 merchants row, 0 of merchant B's ledger), and with the broad `merchants_customer_read` policy also restored a plain shopper read another merchant's `account_balance` and `phone` off the base table | **Founder ruling 2026-08-24: record and DEFER.** Do not repair this by restoring broad SELECT on `merchants` — this audit demonstrated that doing so resurrects D147's critical wallet/PII leak. It fails closed today; resolve it later with a deliberately designed isolation mechanism, **not before Merchant 01**. The design when it is taken up: a SECURITY DEFINER helper (e.g. `public.user_owns_merchant(uuid)`) substituted into the four `USING` clauses, mirroring how `current_user_id()` and `merchant_verify_authorized()` already solve this exact problem — it restores the policies with no base-table grant. **Deliberately not applied by the 2026-08-24 audit**: MEDIUM severity is record-first under the audit brief, it is a migration against RLS on money tables, and Node 0 field validation is in progress. Any future column-scoped `GRANT SELECT (col, ...)` on `merchants` must keep `browse_views_test.sql` Scenarios H/I/J green. no guard: no existing suite asserts that a policy's referenced tables are readable by the roles the policy applies to; a scenario walking `pg_policies` for cross-table references and checking grant reachability would be the durable one, and is the recommended companion to the fix | eng |
| D169 | open | code-outlier | 2026-08-24 | Security / grants | **A fresh deploy from this repo grants `authenticated` INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE on `public.merchant_transactions` — the wallet ledger — and production does not.** Found by building a fresh database from all 100 migrations and diffing the anon/authenticated grant surface against `axrrslqssmbngbataejg` on 2026-08-24: the diff is exactly three rows, all of them `merchant_transactions` / `authenticated` / INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE, present in fresh and absent in production. Cause: `20260723120000_revoke_authenticated_writes_core_tables.sql` strips those grants from `merchants`, `deals` and `redemptions` and **not** from `merchant_transactions`, while Supabase's default privileges grant ALL on every new table in `public`; `20260720130000` explicitly classes `merchant_transactions` as a user-facing table and leaves it alone too. **Not exploitable in either database** — the table has only SELECT policies, so RLS default-deny refuses the write. Proved on the fresh DB by attempting a fabricated KES 999,999 top-up as an authenticated merchant owner: `new row violates row-level security policy for table "merchant_transactions"`. What the row records is that repo ≠ production on a money table, in the direction where the repo is weaker: a rebuild, DR restore, staging clone or second node would come up holding write grants production does not have, with nothing between that grant and arbitrary ledger writes except the continued absence of any INSERT policy on the table | **Founder ruling 2026-08-24: FIX REQUIRED BEFORE THE NEXT PRODUCTION MIGRATION IS DEPLOYED.** Production is secure; a fresh deployment is weaker, and that is dangerous configuration drift to leave latent on a money table. It does not delay Merchant 01 — it gates the *next* migration, and must ship with a ratchet. One-line fix, in the shape the repo already uses: `REVOKE INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE ON public.merchant_transactions FROM authenticated;` plus a scenario in `maanta-app/supabase/tests/revoke_authenticated_writes_core_tables_test.sql` asserting the grant is absent — which would also make the repo the thing that guarantees production's current posture rather than something production happens to have. **Deliberately not applied by the 2026-08-24 audit**: MEDIUM is record-first, and it is a production migration during Node 0 field validation. Note the fix does not change production behaviour at all — it makes a future fresh deploy match what production already is | eng |
| D170 | open | product-decision | 2026-08-24 | Merchant staff / consent | **A merchant can enroll any shopper as staff knowing only their email address, and the shopper's role changes on next sign-in without being asked.** `getMerchantContext()` (`maanta-app/src/lib/merchant.ts`) links an unclaimed `merchant_staff` seat to the signed-in user by exact match on `users.email` (D154) or `users.phone`, then, when `user.role === 'customer'`, writes `role = 'merchant_staff'`. The invite route defaults `can_verify` to true (`canVerify !== false`). So a merchant who types a shopper's address into their staff list captures that shopper on their next sign-in: the shopper's role flips and they gain the inviting merchant's counter surface, including that merchant's wallet balance, arrears, staff list and redemptions. **This is not a cross-tenant breach** — everything exposed belongs to the merchant who chose to issue the invite — and three real limits hold: an owner is immune (the owned-merchant branch returns before staff linking), an already-linked staff member is immune (`user_id` is matched first), and a non-customer role such as `admin` is never rewritten. Both linking branches also fail closed on ambiguity: `.maybeSingle()` errors when two unclaimed seats share an address, so `byEmail` comes back null and nothing links. What is missing is the invitee's consent — every comparable invite system requires the invitee to accept, and here first sign-in is acceptance | **Founder ruling 2026-08-24: staff acceptance is REQUIRED before access becomes active.** An owner may invite an email, but the invited person must not silently become merchant staff merely because their address exists in MAANTA — especially once beyond the controlled pilot. **Scope of the ruling:** for Merchant 01 / Staff 01 the pilot may operate under explicit in-person consent, recorded operationally; the productized acceptance flow is required **before scaled staff-seat issuance**, not before the first controlled test. So this row does not block Merchant 01 and is not implemented in the audit PR. Open question left with the flow's designer: whether `can_verify` should continue to default to on. Recorded now because D154 made email a linking key and email addresses are far easier to guess than phone numbers, which changes the reachability of this path even though the mechanism predates it. no guard: the behaviour is intended as built, so a test would pin the current design rather than catch a regression; the guard belongs with whatever the ruling decides | founder |
| D171 | open | code-outlier | 2026-08-24 | Fraud / enforcement | **`users.is_blacklisted` is enforced nowhere — the admin console shows it, and nothing acts on it.** The column exists on `public.users` from the baseline migration and the admin surfaces render it as a status chip (`maanta-app/src/app/admin/customers/page.tsx`, `.../customers/[id]/page.tsx`). Measured on `axrrslqssmbngbataejg` 2026-08-24: **zero** functions and **zero** RLS policies reference the column — `claim_deal` does not consult it, `verify_redemption` does not, no route checks it, no policy filters on it. An admin who blacklists an abusive shopper during Node 0 would see the label change and nothing else would happen: that shopper can still claim and still redeem. Nobody is relying on it today (0 blacklisted users on production, real or demo), which is the only reason this is not worse. Adjacent and compounding: a `users` row with no foreign-key references can be deleted by its own holder through PostgREST (RLS scopes the delete to self — deleting another user's row affects 0 rows, verified), and the next sign-in re-provisions a fresh row via the `clerk_user_id` upsert — so even an enforced flag would be resettable by an unreferenced account. In practice any shopper worth blacklisting has redemptions, and the `redemptions_user_id_fkey` constraint blocks the delete (verified) | **Founder ruling 2026-08-24: DEFER.** An unenforced `is_blacklisted` is a product/governance inconsistency, not a reason to hold Merchant 01. Decide what blacklisting is actually supposed to *prevent* before wiring it everywhere. When taken up, three coherent options: (a) enforce it where fraud is already handled — a `is_blacklisted` check in `claim_deal`, matching how `is_shadow_banned` already gates the merchant side in the same RPC; (b) remove the column and the chips, and let Guardian plus `review_required` be the whole shopper-side story, which is what actually runs today; (c) leave it, and relabel the admin chip so it does not read as an enforced block. What must not persist is a control-shaped label that controls nothing. no guard: there is nothing to regress against until the ruling picks a behaviour; the guard belongs with option (a) if that is chosen — a SQL scenario asserting a blacklisted user's `claim_deal` raises | founder |
| D172 | deferred | process | 2026-08-24 | CI / test coverage | **The golden-path browser E2E has never executed — 167 workflow runs, every one skipped, because `E2E_BASE_URL` was never provisioned.** *Originally and temporarily recorded as **D168** in Notion (Decisions Log, 2026-08-23 late; also quoted on Current State). Renumbered to **D172** by founder ruling 2026-08-24 after it collided with canonical repo **D168** (inert tenant-isolation RLS policies), which was merged to `main` first and is unchanged. The repo is canonical for drift IDs; the E2E finding had never been landed as a register row on any ref.* **Deferred by founder ruling the same day — it does not block Merchant 01; it gates the move to routine/scaled releases (see Owner).** **Verified independently against `main` @ `59160b1`, 2026-08-24.** `.github/workflows/e2e.yml` gates its only job on `if: vars.E2E_BASE_URL != '' && github.ref == 'refs/heads/main'`, so with the repository variable unset the job is a no-op. Run history read in full via the Actions API — **167/167 runs `skipped`, zero executions**, from run #1 (2026-07-24, `pull_request`) to run #167 (2026-08-24 08:40 UTC on `59160b1`, the PR #265 merge). No run has ever reached `success` or `failure`. **Three corrections to how this was first written, all narrowing it.** (1) **The count is 167, not 166** — it rises by one on every push to `main`, so any fixed number in a document goes stale immediately; cite the gate, not the tally. (2) **On canonical `main` it is the golden-path money loop, not a "dashboard" suite.** `maanta-app/e2e/golden-path.spec.ts` is the ONLY spec on `main`; it covers shopper browse → claim → 6-digit ticket → merchant verify → KES 30 fee/arrears → wallet, plus a negative invalid/expired-code case. It is authenticated (signed-in shopper and merchant Playwright storage states) but asserts nothing about any dashboard. **In fairness to the original wording: `maanta-app/e2e/dashboards.spec.ts` does exist on PR #264's branch (`claude/d162-d164`, open, unmerged), so "dashboard E2E" was accurate for the branch that finding was written against — it is simply not what canonical `main` holds today.** Part of why the collision went unnoticed is that the finding was scoped to an unmerged branch's contents. (3) **"MAANTA has never had authenticated E2E testing" is too strong and is refuted.** What is missing is the *browser-level* proof. The same money invariants are gated in CI at the RPC level by `supabase/tests/golden_path_test.sql` and `verify_redemption_money_path_test.sql` (the spec's own header says so), alongside 1061 vitest tests including route-handler and Clerk-strategy smoke coverage. **This is an unprovisioned opt-in gate, not a broken or deceptive one.** The workflow header states the design explicitly — "stays OFF until an operator provisions it", "Without E2E_BASE_URL the job is a no-op ... so it never runs prematurely" — and two guards already exist against a false green: a `Require storage states (fail a zero-coverage run)` step that **fails** rather than passes when the secrets are absent, and a `test.skip` in the spec so a local run can never report false coverage. The residual risk is therefore honest-but-absent coverage, not silent green. | `.github/workflows/e2e.yml` (the `if:` gate, the non-prod target guard, the zero-coverage preflight); `maanta-app/e2e/golden-path.spec.ts`; `docs/ops/e2e-golden-path.md` (the provisioning runbook); Actions run history for `e2e.yml` read in full 2026-08-24 (167 runs, all `skipped`). no guard: this is an ops-provisioning gap, not a code defect — nothing in the repo can assert that a repository variable and two environment secrets exist in GitHub settings, and a test that tried would either pass vacuously or fail every fork. | founder / ops — tracker **E14**. **Founder ruling 2026-08-24 (third entry): D172 does NOT block Merchant 01, and is DEFERRED with a trigger.** The precise state is a three-way distinction, not a binary: MAANTA **does** have automated database/RPC money-path coverage; it **has had** a successful manual browser E2E; what it does **not** have is an **automated browser E2E actually executing in CI**. There is already substantially more direct production evidence than a one-merchant controlled pilot requires, and making browser E2E mandatory now would return the project to engineering when the remaining uncertainty is market behaviour. **Trigger — this becomes a hard gate before MAANTA moves from controlled Node 0 validation into routine or scaled releases.** The reasoning is about frequency and dependence: once deploys are routine and genuine customers depend on MAANTA, every production release should prove the golden path automatically rather than relying on someone remembering to run it by hand. Close condition unchanged: provision the `e2e` environment, set `E2E_BASE_URL` to a dedicated **non-prod** deployment, add `E2E_SHOPPER_STORAGE` and `E2E_MERCHANT_STORAGE`, then record one run that actually executes. **No implementation task is authorized yet** — the 2026-08-24 renumbering was governance only, and the founder ruled explicitly that Claude is not to be given a D172 implementation task at this stage. |

Attribution: **27 Elite** merchants at 3 active — 2 seed-batch rows plus 1 `autoreseed` flash row, where `reseed_demo_flash_deals()` legitimately inserted while under cap (it deactivates expired demo deals first to free allowance) and the nightly `refresh_demo_seed_deals()` then blindly reactivated both batch rows on top; and **1 Standard** merchant at 2 active from the same blanket reactivation. **All 28 are demo merchants; no genuine merchant was ever over cap** (2 non-demo active deals, 0 over cap), so the commercial rule was never breached in the field — but the same gap would apply to any future merchant-facing "reactivate a paused deal" action, and it left the founder ruling of 2026-08-26 (*no paused deal may be reactivated over the cap*) with no database enforcement behind it | Fix authored 2026-08-27 on branch `claude/d206-cap-update-guard`, **not yet applied to production**: migration `maanta-app/supabase/migrations/20260827120000_cap_enforce_on_slot_entry.sql` makes the trigger `BEFORE INSERT OR UPDATE` but fires only on ENTRY INTO OCCUPANCY (`OLD.is_active = FALSE AND NEW.is_active = TRUE`), so a merchant at cap can still edit, pause, extend or archive the deal that owns its slot; flash stays Elite-only including on reactivation; no `is_demo` exemption; INSERT behaviour unchanged. `refresh_demo_seed_deals()` is rewritten cap-compliant BY CONSTRUCTION — it subtracts slots held by rows it does not manage, fills the remaining allowance in a stable `created_at, id` order, skips flash rows for Standard merchants and retires the surplus, so the next scheduled run repairs the existing over-cap state without anyone editing production by hand. Guards: `maanta-app/supabase/tests/deal_limit_cap_test.sql` scenarios H–L and `maanta-app/supabase/tests/demo_seed_refresh_cap_test.sql` A–E, verified by four mutants (naive `INSERT OR UPDATE` freezing merchants out, revert to INSERT-only, blanket demo reactivation, and an unqualified scratch-table reference) each failing the suites. The fourth came out of the substitute security review run when both automated review gates were unavailable on PR #284: the rewritten `refresh_demo_seed_deals()` uses a temp table, and because the function pins `search_path = public, pg_temp` an explicitly-listed `pg_temp` is searched LAST — so an unqualified `_refresh_keep` resolves to `public._refresh_keep` first. Demonstrated, not theorised: the unqualified draft DROPPED an unrelated `public._refresh_keep` table from inside a SECURITY DEFINER body. Every reference is now `pg_temp.`-qualified and scenario E holds it there. **A second review finding, from Codex on the same PR and confirmed by reproduction:** the documented seed rerun path (`AGENTS.md` — apply `maanta-app/supabase/seed/node0_100_deals_seed.sql`) ends in a blanket `UPDATE ... SET is_active = true` over its whole id range, which under the new guard **aborts the entire seed transaction** as soon as one of those merchants has a slot held by a row outside the range — an `autoreseed` flash deal is exactly that. Measured: `Deal limit reached. elite plan allows 2 active deal(s).` Both that seed and `maanta-app/supabase/seed/node0_rehearsal_seed.sql` now use the same allowance-based selection as `refresh_demo_seed_deals()` — already-active rows refreshed unconditionally, inactive rows activated only within the slots left, oldest id first — verified before and after on the same probe state (blanket form aborts, fixed form completes and lands at exactly cap). **APPLIED to production 2026-08-27** under founder authorization, after PR #284 merged as `5aaf522` (tree-verified against the audited head `87ce088`). The apply minted `20260827074843` — eleven for eleven — repaired to the repo filename before anything else; **ledger 105/105**, high-water `20260827120000`. Read back: trigger fires `INSERT UPDATE`, transition check live, refresh `pg_temp.`-qualified and cap-aware, exactly one overload each, EXECUTE still `postgres`/`service_role` only, cron `30 2 * * *` active. A live negative test on production, written to roll itself back, confirmed all three behaviours on real data: `reactivate_over_cap = [Deal limit reached. elite plan allows 2 active deal(s).]`, `edit_at_cap = [allowed]`, `leave_occupancy = [allowed]`; row counts identical before and after (2408 deals / 331 active / 215 merchants / 404 redemptions / 0 tier_flags). **Census still reads 28 over cap and that is correct** — the migration changes behaviour, not rows, per the ruling not to clean production rows by hand. **Still not closed**: closure is the 02:30 UTC scheduled `maanta_demo_seed_refresh` on 2026-08-28 succeeding, taking over-cap 28 to 0 and not recreating it, with total active deals landing at **303** — 261 batch rows kept of 289 active (28 retired, exactly the 28 over-cap merchants) plus 40 `autoreseed` plus 2 genuine, measured on production. An earlier draft of the ops doc set that target at 288; Codex caught it on PR #285 and it was wrong twice over — it counted only the batch and assumed a single dropped row, ignoring the 40 slots `autoreseed` already holds. A 288 target would have accepted the silent loss of ~15 deals or made a correct run look wrong. **CLOSED 2026-08-28 by the scheduled run, measured read-only.** `cron.job_run_details` runid 769: **succeeded**, 02:30:00.095767+00 -> 02:30:00.495429+00, `1 row` — the first scheduled run under the new trigger did NOT abort, which was the whole risk of the rewrite. Census after it: **over_cap_total 0** (genuine 0, demo 0), Standard max **1**, Elite max **2**. The run set 258 rows active and 29 inactive; all 29 retirements were Elite merchants at 3 active losing exactly 1 to land at 2 — **no merchant was cut below its cap** and no Standard merchant lost a deal. The over-cap state was repaired by the function itself, with no hand-editing of production rows. **Supply landed at 300 active, not the 303 this row predicted, and the prediction was the thing that was wrong.** 303 assumed one retirement per over-cap merchant (289 - 28 = 261 batch); the function instead allocates per merchant — cap minus foreign-held slots, filled in stable `created_at, id` order. Recomputing that rule independently from the merchant/deal tables predicts **258** batch rows and matches observed state for **208 of 208** batch merchants, zero deviations. Reconciliation of the 289 batch rows: 258 kept + 29 retired + 2 already inactive. Those 2 are expired flash rows deactivated by `reseed_demo_flash_deals()`, whose retire step never sets `updated_at` (confirmed via `pg_get_functiondef`) — pre-existing, unrelated to D206, and the reason the pre-run batch figure was 287 not 289. Neither was eligible to return: one is a Standard merchant's flash row (D207's row, refused by the Elite-only rule), the other sits behind a fuller allowance. Not starved: 272 shopper-visible in `deals_public_browse`, dark fixture shops still **0**, and the 13 Elite merchants at 1/2 own only one batch deal each (0 promotable rows) — supply, not the guard. Ledger **105/105**, high-water `20260827120000`, full version+name read-back md5-identical to the repo directory (`608e0dabc573bf0f0226ae83ebf50ec1`). | D208 | open | code-outlier | 2026-08-27 | `maanta-app/src/app/admin/page.tsx` success-fee sum | **`/admin` sums success fees the way D149 says not to.** It pulls `merchant_transactions` rows into JS and `.reduce()`s them, while `/admin/reports` and `/founder` deliberately call the `admin_success_fee_revenue` RPC precisely because PostgREST's 1000-row cap silently truncates the row set and under-reports the SUM. Same money figure, two methods, and the app-layer one fails quietly and low — the worst direction for a revenue number. Harmless today at 3 fee rows in all of production, which is exactly why it has survived; it becomes wrong without warning somewhere past 1000 fee rows. Found while building PR 5, which needs per-merchant fee sums the global RPC cannot provide | **Not fixed in PR 5 — out of its locked scope**, and the fix is a judgement call rather than a mechanism: either a node/merchant-scoped RPC (a migration, so founder-authorized) or a bounded read that reports UNAVAILABLE at the cap. | D209 | open | code-outlier | 2026-08-27 | `maanta-app/src/lib/ui.ts` `friendlyTime()` | **D190 was fixed on one screen; the same defect is still live on every merchant, admin and agent surface.** `friendlyTime()` formats with `toLocaleTimeString("en-KE")` and compares days with `toDateString()`, both in the **server's** timezone — UTC on Vercel, three hours behind Nairobi. So "Today, 10:42am" renders as "Today, 7:42am", and between 21:00 and 24:00 EAT the day word is wrong outright: a redemption at 22:30 Tuesday says "Today" to a server that still thinks it is Tuesday 19:30, then flips to "Yesterday" three hours early. | D211 | open | product-decision | 2026-08-28 | Fee KPI reversal semantics | **The success-fee KPI measures GROSS linked fee entries, and nothing on the surface says so.** PR 5 moved both fee figures off `redemptions.success_fee_charged` and onto the ledger, joined by `reference_id` over `success_fee` and `success_fee_arrears` — correct, because `verify_redemption` commits a successful redemption even when the fee step throws, leaving a fee amount on the row that never reached the ledger. Each described the defect as intended behaviour, which is what a later session reads before changing that code. **Prose-scanning guards are not an acceptable solution and are ruled out** — three were written during PR #288 and all three failed: one read the wrong file (D38 vacuity), one treated a missing file as success so a rename was a permanent blind spot, and the third encoded a blacklist of the exact sentences already observed, which trivial variants pass | **Not fixed — the guard was REMOVED from PR #288 by founder ruling 2026-08-28** rather than rewritten a fourth time inside a release gate; `drift-register.test.ts` is back to its pre-PR state. The remedy is structural: encode what a record ASSERTS in a machine-checkable form that can be validated against git and against the code it describes — a merge commit SHA and both parents, the ids renumbered and from what — rather than matching sentences. A phrase list cannot express the invariant, which is that the record agrees with reality. **Scope: this row is about durable-record validation only.** It is NOT a bucket for brittle guards in general — the two mechanism-pinned shopper guards removed on 2026-08-28 were deleted outright and left no surviving gap. Design deliberately and outside a merge gate. | D213 | open | code-outlier | 2026-08-28 | Shopper time-decay: `/feed` "Ending soon", `/my-deals` Fast Visit chip, horizontal `DealCard` | **Server-rendered time-sensitive claims go stale on a page left open, and two of them assert something false rather than merely out of date.** `/my-deals` is a server component with no tick: `fastVisitChipState` is evaluated once at render, so a row keeps asserting "Fast Visit open" after `claimed_at + 15 minutes` has passed. `/feed` computes `endingSoonDeals(allDeals)` once during its server render, so a deal that expires while the feed is open remains under a heading that says "Ending soon". The horizontal `DealCard` variant compounds it by rendering the server-computed static `expiryLabel`, while the tall variants already use the ticking `CountdownChip` (30s interval) — an inconsistency that PREDATES PR #288 and is not a regression of it. Both surfaces were verified live during review; the ticking machinery already exists, so this is a design decision about these surfaces rather than a missing capability | **Deferred by founder ruling 2026-08-28 — a controlled deferral, not a dismissal.** Closing it means introducing client reactivity across `/feed` and `/my-deals`, a cross-surface change deliberately kept out of PR #288 after that PR was narrowed to shopper clarity. **Acceptance criteria, set by the founder so the deferral cannot soften. Criteria 1 and 2 are unchanged from 2026-08-28; criterion 3 was WIDENED and criterion 4 ADDED the same day, after review evidence showed the original three could all pass with the class still live: (1)** the Fast Visit chip closes when its deadline passes on an open page, without navigation or refresh; **(2)** expired deals leave the "Ending soon" collection without navigation or refresh; **(3)** every shopper-rendered variant is self-consistent — labels, countdown chips, section membership and claimability must not contradict one another as time passes on an open page; **(4)** inventory exhaustion is reflected: when the final available claim is taken, an already-open shopper surface stops advertising the deal as available **within one defined refresh interval — 30 seconds unless implementation establishes a stricter bound.** Criterion 3 was widened from "horizontal expiry labels" because the **vertical** `DealCard` renders a ticking `CountdownChip` AND the static `expiryLabel` on one card (`deal-card.tsx` 247 and 257), and `/browse` passes both (`src/components/browse/browse-client.tsx:229`, `variant="vertical"`) — so the narrow wording could be satisfied in full while a live card contradicted itself. Criterion 4 exists because the `c52133e` claim-cap filter runs once per server render: a deal capped by another shopper while the page is open kept being advertised, and criteria 1–3 said nothing about it. Both criteria cover the defect wherever it lives, not only where PR #288 surfaced it. Note the claim-cap exclusion added in `c52133e` is a SEPARATE concern and does not address any of the three criteria — a capped deal is now excluded at render time, but membership still does not decay while the page is open. **Both cases below were verified on 2026-08-28 and were NOT covered by the original three criteria. The founder ruled the same day that they must be: (a) is now criterion 3 as widened, (b) is now criterion 4.** (a) The **vertical** `DealCard` renders a ticking `CountdownChip` AND the static `expiryLabel` on the same card (`deal-card.tsx` lines 247 and 257), and `/browse` passes both (`src/components/browse/browse-client.tsx:229`, `variant="vertical"`) — so one card carries two claims about one deal and only one of them decays. Criterion 3 **as originally written** named horizontal labels only, so it could have been satisfied while `/browse` stayed stale — which is why it was widened the same day. (b) A deal that reaches its cap **while a page is already open** is not removed: `claims_count` and `endingSoon` membership are computed once per server render, so the card remains under "Claim windows closing within the hour" though `claim_deal` now raises `deal_claim_limit_reached`. The `c52133e` filter only excludes deals already capped at render time. Under the original three criteria, all of them could pass with (a) and (b) still live — which is why they were widened rather than left frozen. Evidence: PR #288 threads on `c0130a1`; `maanta-app/src/lib/fast-visit-chip.ts`, `maanta-app/src/lib/ending-soon.ts`, `maanta-app/src/components/ui/claude/deal-card.tsx`, `maanta-app/src/components/ui/chips.tsx` | founder |
| D214 | closed | code-outlier | 2026-08-28 | `maanta-app/src/app/(shopper)/shops/[id]/page.tsx` | **The shop storefront listed PAUSED deals under a heading that says "Live deals".** The frozen paused-deal rule (**D25**) is that pausing removes a deal from shopper discovery immediately, and CLAUDE.md stated `/search` was the only surface that had ever missed it (**D119**, closed 2026-08-19). That was wrong: `shops/[id]/page.tsx` builds its own `deals` select rather than reading `getLiveDeals`, filtering `is_active`, `expires_at > now` and `is_demo` but **not** `is_paused` — the identical omission, in the identical shape, on a second surface. A merchant who paused a deal still had it advertised on their own storefront under "Live deals". **Following the card did NOT normally produce a 409** — `/deals/[id]` reads `is_paused`, sets `claimable` false and renders the disabled "Deal paused by merchant" state instead of mounting `ClaimFlow`; the `deal_paused` 409 is the stale/deep-link path CLAUDE.md already describes. The defect is the false discovery claim, not a broken claim attempt. **A third surface had the same omission**: `(shopper)/notifications/page.tsx` selected `deals` on `is_active` + `created_at` only, so a merchant who posted and then paused within 24h kept being advertised as "New deal from a saved shop". Found while narrowing the discoverable-is-not-claimable doctrine, whose claim that paused deals are "withdrawn from discovery entirely" was false while these stood | **Fixed in the same change**: the query now carries `.eq("is_paused", false)` beside the existing liveness filters, with the D25/D119 reasoning inline. Two mutants proven to fail: removing the predicate restores the defect, and swapping `is_active` for it trades one discovery leak for another. **The general lesson is D119's, not this row's: a shopper surface that builds its own deal query instead of reading `getLiveDeals` will miss a predicate the shared path carries.** Three surfaces have now done it, so this was audited as a class rather than patched instance by instance: every direct `from("deals")` select in the app was checked. The shopper-discovery set is `getLiveDeals`, `/search` (D119), `shops/[id]` and `/notifications` — all four now carry `is_paused`. The merchant, admin and founder surfaces are deliberately OUTSIDE the rule and were left alone; `merchant/(app)/redeem` filters `is_paused = true` on purpose, because a merchant must see their own paused deals. **Two further omissions on `/notifications` were found by the same audit and are NOT fixed here.** Founder ruling 2026-08-28: they are split into their own separately traceable pre-launch rows rather than folded into this paused-deal repair, because they are different rules with different failure modes — **D215** (no merchant-visibility gate) and **D216** (launch mode does not exclude demo deals). Evidence: `maanta-app/src/lib/__tests__/shop-page-paused-filter.test.ts`, `maanta-app/src/lib/__tests__/notifications-paused-filter.test.ts`, `maanta-app/src/lib/__tests__/search-paused-filter.test.ts` | claude |
| D215 | open | code-outlier | 2026-08-28 | `maanta-app/src/app/(shopper)/notifications/page.tsx` | **The notifications inbox applies no merchant-visibility gate, so a suspended or shadow-banned merchant can still reach shoppers.** Every other shopper surface reads merchant availability through `withPublicMerchant` / `withPublicMerchantRows`, which pin `status = 'active'`, `is_visible = true` and `is_shadow_banned = false` — the same three conditions `claim_deal` enforces as `merchant_not_available`, and the read-side contract D123/D147 hardened. Shadow-ban is the sharper case: its whole purpose is that the merchant does not know they are hidden, and an inbox notification tells them otherwise the moment a shopper mentions it | **Not fixed in PR #290 — split out by founder ruling 2026-08-28** so it is separately traceable rather than folded into the paused-deal repair, which is a different rule. Found during the D214 audit of every direct `from("deals")` select. **Pre-launch**: Merchant 01's onboarding at Node 0 involves real suspension and approval states, so a notification escaping the visibility gate would reach a real shopper. The fix is to route this read through the shared helper rather than to re-hand-write the three conditions — a second place to enforce the rule is a second place to drift. **But it must thread `isDemoModeEnabled()` into the helper, and D215 and D216 must be implemented together.** `withPublicMerchant`'s default path adds `is_demo = false` AND `merchants.is_demo = false` when `opts.includeDemo` is falsy (`data.ts` 299-304), so adopting the helper naively would suppress these notifications **even with demo mode ON** — over-correcting past D216, which requires them to keep working in that mode. Closing D215 alone, exactly as a careless reading of this row would prescribe, would therefore introduce a new defect rather than only fixing one. Evidence: `maanta-app/src/lib/merchant-visibility.ts`, `maanta-app/src/lib/data.ts` `withPublicMerchant`, `maanta-app/src/lib/__tests__/visibility.test.ts` | founder |
| D216 | open | code-outlier | 2026-08-28 | `maanta-app/src/app/(shopper)/notifications/page.tsx` | **Launch-mode notifications do not exclude demo deals, so a synthetic deal can notify a real shopper.** The frozen rule is that no synthetic row renders unless demo mode is on, and every other shopper read threads `includeDemo` from `isDemoModeEnabled()` and filters `is_demo` when it is false — `shops/[id]` carries the filter per-row precisely so the rule holds even if a demo deal ever hangs off a non-demo merchant. This page reads neither: it applies no `is_demo` predicate in either mode, so with demo mode OFF a synthetic deal from a favourited demo merchant still produces "New deal from a saved shop". **This is the D189 contamination shape**, where a prospect claimed a synthetic deal within ~8 hours and it landed in the non-demo count via D188 — except arriving in an inbox, which is a push toward the deal rather than a passive listing | **Not fixed in PR #290 — split out by founder ruling 2026-08-28**, separately traceable from the merchant-visibility gap (D215) because they are different rules with different failure modes. **Demo-mode behaviour must remain explicit**: with demo mode ON these notifications are correct and wanted, since the marketplace doubles as a sales-demonstration surface (founder ruling 2026-08-26); the defect is only that launch mode does not exclude them. So the fix reads `isDemoModeEnabled()` and filters on the result rather than hardcoding either answer. **Pre-launch**: demo mode must be OFF for Merchant 01's onboarding and Shopper 01's claim, and that is exactly the configuration in which this leaks. ---

## How to add a row

1. Take the next unused ID. Never reuse one.
2. State the gap as **claim vs reality**, not as a task. "X says A, code does B" ages
   correctly; "fix X" does not.
3. If you are opening and closing it in the same change, still add the row — the
   register is the record that it was ever wrong.
4. Cite evidence as a repo-root-relative path in backticks. The test resolves it.
5. Bump `Last updated` at the top. The test checks it is not older than the newest
   row.
